The purpose of this section is to derive the self-similar form of the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetimes in SSCNG, that is, coordinates
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
in which the metric takes the SSC form (
5.29
) such that the time coordinate is normalized to proper time (recall this means
B(\bar{t},0)=1
). Even though we only fully address the instability of
SM
in the case of zero pressure,
\sigma=0
, for completeness, and future reference, we derive self-similar forms of the
k=0
Friedmann spacetimes in the more general case
p=\sigma\rho
with
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
. We show that the
k=0
Friedmann metric is self-similar in the sense that, when written in SSCNG, the metric components and fluid variables
v
and
\rho\bar{r}^{2}
depend only on the self-similar variable
\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}
. In other words, as a function of
(\bar{t},\xi)
, the SSCNG components of the
k=0
Friedmann spacetimes are time independent. Our goal is to study the stability of the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetimes by deriving the equations for spherically symmetric spacetimes in SSCNG coordinates expressed in variables
(t,\xi)
. We Taylor expand the equations about the origin
\xi=0
and obtain a phase portrait with the knowledge ahead of time that the
k=0
Friedmann spacetimes must show up as a rest point in these coordinates.

We begin by stating Theorem 2, on page 88 of
[
26
]
regarding the
k=0
Friedmann spacetime with
p=\sigma\rho
and
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
. Note that typically
0\leq\sigma\leq\frac{1}{3}c^{2}
and we set
c=1
when convenient.

Theorem 29
.

In comoving coordinates, the
k=0
Friedmann metric takes the standard form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-dt^{2}+R(t)^{2}\big(dr^{2}+r^{2}d\Omega^{2}\big)

(6.1)

and the Einstein field equations

\displaystyle G=\kappa T

(6.2)

for a perfect fluid

\displaystyle T=(\rho+p)\vec{u}\otimes\vec{u}+pg

(6.3)

reduce to the following system of ODE:

\displaystyle H^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{3}\rho,

(6.4)

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}

\displaystyle=-3(\rho+p)H,

(6.5)

where

\displaystyle H=\frac{\dot{R}}{R}

is the Hubble constant,
p
is the pressure,
\rho
is the energy density and
\vec{u}
is the fluid four-velocity.

In the case
p=\sigma\rho
with constant
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
(equivalently
\frac{2}{3}\leq\alpha\leq\frac{4}{3}
), assuming
\dot{R}>0
,
R(0)=0
and
R(t_{0})=1
, equations (
6.4
)–(
6.5
) determine
R(t)
to be

\displaystyle R(t)=\left(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3(1+\sigma)}}=\left(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},

(6.6)

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{4}{3(1+\sigma)}.

The resulting solution of (
6.4
)–(
6.5
) is given by:

\displaystyle H(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3(1+\sigma)}\frac{1}{t},

(6.7)

\displaystyle\rho(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3\kappa(1+\sigma)^{2}}\frac{1}{t^{2}},

(6.8)

and by the comoving assumption, the four velocity
\vec{u}
satisfies

\displaystyle\vec{u}=(u^{0},u^{1},u^{2},u^{3})=(1,0,0,0).

Furthermore, the age of the Universe,
t_{0}
, and the infinite red shift limit,
r_{\infty}
, are given exactly by:

\displaystyle t_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3(1+\sigma)}\frac{1}{H_{0}},

\displaystyle r_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+3\sigma}\frac{1}{H_{0}}.

The formulas in Theorem
29
follow directly from (
5.13
)–(
5.17
) and one can easily verify that the free constant
t_{0}
is related to the parameter
\Delta_{0}
defined in (
5.17
) by

\displaystyle t_{0}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{3\kappa(1+\sigma^{2})\Delta_{0}}}.

(6.9)

In particular, when
p=0
we have
\alpha=\frac{4}{3}
, so

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}},

\displaystyle\rho(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3\kappa}\frac{1}{t^{2}},

and when
p=\frac{1}{3}\rho
we have
\alpha=1
, so

\displaystyle R(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},

\displaystyle\rho(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4\kappa}\frac{1}{t^{2}}.

Our goal now is to prove the following theorem, which establishes the unique coordinate system
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
in which the
p=\sigma\rho
,
k=0
Friedmann metric (
6.1
) takes the SSC form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-B(\bar{t},\bar{r})d\bar{t}^{2}+\frac{d\bar{r}^{2}}{A(\bar{t},\bar{r})}+\bar{r}^{2}d\Omega^{2},

such that the metric components
A
and
B
, together with appropriately scaled expressions for the density and velocity, all depend only on the self-similar variable
\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}
. For continuity, we employ the notation of
[
29
]
.

Theorem 30
.

Let
(t,r)
denote comoving coordinates for the
k=0
Friedmann metric (
6.1
) assuming the equation of state
p=\sigma\rho
with constant
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
, and define coordinates
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
by:

\displaystyle\bar{t}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(\eta)t,

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=\eta t=t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}r,

(6.10)

where

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{r}}{t},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2-\alpha}},

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3(1+\sigma)}.

(6.11)

Then the Friedmann metric (
6.1
) written in
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
-coordinates is given by

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-B_{\sigma}d\bar{t}^{2}+\frac{1}{A_{\sigma}}d\bar{r}^{2}+\bar{r}^{2}d\Omega^{2},

(6.12)

where the metric components
A_{\sigma}
,
B_{\sigma}
,
\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}
and

\displaystyle v_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{\sigma}B_{\sigma}}}\frac{\bar{u}^{1}_{\sigma}}{\bar{u}_{\sigma}^{0}}

are functions of the single variable
\eta
according to:

\displaystyle A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=1-\left(\frac{\alpha\eta}{2}\right)^{2},

(6.13)

\displaystyle B_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\right)^{\frac{2-2\alpha}{2-\alpha}}}{1-\left(\frac{\alpha\eta}{2}\right)^{2}},

(6.14)

\displaystyle\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}\alpha^{2}\eta^{2},

(6.15)

\displaystyle v_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\eta.

(6.16)

Moreover,
\eta
is given implicitly as a function of
\xi
by the the relation

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}=\frac{\eta}{\mathcal{F}(\eta)},

(6.17)

which inverts to

\displaystyle\eta^{2}=\xi^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\xi^{4}+\frac{\alpha^{3}}{16}\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8})

(6.18)

by expanding about
\xi=\eta=0
. This determines the following asymptotic expansions for
A_{\sigma}
,
B_{\sigma}
,
\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}
and
v_{\sigma}
as functions of
\xi
in a neighborhood of
\xi=\eta=0
, valid as
\xi\to 0
:

\displaystyle A_{\sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}\xi^{2}-\frac{\alpha^{3}}{8}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.19)

\displaystyle B_{\sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.20)

\displaystyle D_{\sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{A_{\sigma}B_{\sigma}}=1+\frac{\alpha(1-\alpha)}{4}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.21)

\displaystyle\big(\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}\big)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}\alpha^{2}\xi^{2}+\frac{3}{8}\alpha^{3}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.22)

\displaystyle v_{\sigma}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2}\xi\Big(1+\frac{\alpha}{4}\xi^{2}\Big)+O(\xi^{4}).

(6.23)

We record two special cases in the following corollary.

Corollary 31
.

In the case
p=0
,
\alpha=\frac{4}{3}
and we have:

\displaystyle A_{0}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{4}{9}\xi^{2}-\frac{8}{27}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.24)

\displaystyle B_{0}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{2}{9}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.25)

\displaystyle D_{0}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{9}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.26)

\displaystyle\big(\kappa\rho_{0}\bar{r}^{2}\big)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}\xi^{2}+\frac{8}{9}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.27)

\displaystyle v_{0}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}\xi\Big(1+\frac{1}{3}\xi^{2}\Big)+O(\xi^{4}).

(6.28)

In the case
p=\frac{1}{3}\rho
,
\alpha=1
and we have:

\displaystyle A_{\frac{1}{3}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{4}\xi^{2}-\frac{1}{8}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.29)

\displaystyle B_{\frac{1}{3}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{4}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.30)

\displaystyle D_{\frac{1}{3}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+O(\xi^{4}),

(6.31)

\displaystyle\big(\kappa\rho_{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{r}^{2}\big)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}\xi^{2}+\frac{3}{8}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(6.32)

\displaystyle v_{\frac{1}{3}}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\xi\Big(1+\frac{1}{4}\xi^{2}\Big)+O(\xi^{4}).

(6.33)

The proof of Theorem
30
is given in Section
13.2
below.

7
The STV-PDE

In this section we derive a new form of the Einstein field equations for spherically symmetric spacetimes in SSCNG coordinates
(t,r)
, that is, coordinates in which the metric takes the form (
1.1
). We do this by re-expressing the Einstein field equations in terms of self-similar variables
(t,\xi)
, where
\xi=\frac{r}{t}
. We call the resulting equations the STV-PDE.
19
19

19

These were introduced by Smoller, Temple and Vogler in
[
29
]
.
Since from here on we only work with solutions in SSCNG, for ease of notation, and for the rest of the paper, we drop the bars from the SSC, the notation employed in Sections
5
to
7
. There should be no confusion when we refer back to Sections
5
to
7
in which
(t,r)
refers to Friedmann comoving coordinates and where bars appear on the SSCNG coordinates (to distinguish them from comoving coordinates).

We start with the equations for spherically symmetric solutions of the Einstein field equations
G=\kappa T
in SSC, now denoted
(t,r)
, derived in
[
12
]
, and look to express them in terms of
(t,\xi)
as the independent variables. Note that this is not the same as writing the Einstein field equations in
(t,\xi)
-coordinates. We then study the subset of solutions of these equations which meet the further condition that solutions be smooth at the center (
r=0
).

In the unbarred notation, a time dependent metric taking the SSC form is given by

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-B(t,r)dt^{2}+\frac{dr^{2}}{A(t,r)}+r^{2}d\Omega^{2},

where

\displaystyle d\Omega^{2}=d\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta d\theta^{2}

is the usual line element on the unit sphere, see
[
34
]
. Then according to
[
12
]
, three of the four Einstein field equations determined by
G=\kappa T
are first order and one is second order. The first order equations are equivalent to:
20
20

20

Metric entries
(A,B)
are related to
(\hat{A},\hat{B})
in
[
12
]
by
A=\frac{1}{\hat{B}}
,
B=\hat{A}
.

\displaystyle-r\frac{A_{r}}{A}+\frac{1-A}{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa B}{A}T^{00}r^{2}=\frac{\kappa}{A}T^{00}_{M}r^{2},

(7.1)

\displaystyle\frac{A_{t}}{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa B}{A}T^{01}r=\kappa\sqrt{\frac{B}{A}}T^{01}_{M}r,

(7.2)

\displaystyle r\frac{B_{r}}{B}-\frac{1-A}{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{A^{2}}T^{11}r^{2}=\frac{\kappa}{A}T^{11}_{M}r^{2},

(7.3)

and the the two conservation laws,
\nabla\cdot T=0
, are equivalent to:

\displaystyle\big(T^{00}_{M}\big)_{t}+\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{01}_{M}\big)_{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{r}\sqrt{AB}T^{01}_{M},

(7.4)

\displaystyle\big(T^{01}_{M}\big)_{t}+\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{11}_{M}\big)_{r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{AB}\bigg(\frac{4}{r}T^{11}_{M}+\frac{1}{r}\Big(\frac{1}{A}-1\Big)\big(T^{00}_{M}-T^{11}_{M}\big)+\frac{2\kappa r}{A}\big(T^{00}_{M}T^{11}_{M}-(T^{01}_{M})^{2}\big)-4rT^{22}\bigg),

(7.5)

where
T_{M}
is the Minkowski stress tensor defined by (see
[
12
]
):

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}

\displaystyle=BT^{00},

\displaystyle T^{01}_{M}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{B}{A}}T^{01},

\displaystyle T^{11}_{M}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{A}T^{11},

\displaystyle T^{22}_{M}

\displaystyle=T^{22}.

Equations (
7.4
)–(
7.5
) are redundant because
\nabla\cdot T=0
follows from
G=\kappa T
by the Bianchi identities. Moreover, to close the equations we must impose an equation of state
[
12
]
. In
[
12
]
it is shown that the Einstein field equations
G=\kappa T
for metrics in SSC are (weakly) equivalent to (
7.1
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
) and (
7.5
), and equation (
7.2
) is derivable from these.

In this paper we assume the equation of state

\displaystyle p

\displaystyle=\sigma\rho

(7.6)

with constant
0\leq\sigma\leq 1
. With this equation of state, the Minkowski stress tensors become:

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\rho\left(\frac{1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{c^{2}}}{1-\frac{v^{2}}{c^{2}}}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{c^{2}}\right)=\rho\frac{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}{1-v^{2}},

(7.7)

\displaystyle T^{01}_{M}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\rho\frac{1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{c^{2}}}{1-\frac{v^{2}}{c^{2}}}\frac{v}{c},

(7.8)

\displaystyle T^{11}_{M}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\rho\left(\frac{1+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{c^{2}}}{1-\frac{v^{2}}{c^{2}}}\frac{v^{2}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{c^{2}}\right)=\rho\frac{\sigma^{2}+v^{2}}{1-v^{2}},

(7.9)

\displaystyle T^{22}_{M}

\displaystyle=pg^{22}=\rho\frac{\sigma^{2}}{r^{2}},

(7.10)

which imply:

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}T^{11}_{M}-\big(T^{01}_{M}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\rho\bigg(\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}-\sigma^{2}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}v^{2}+\sigma^{2}\bigg)-\bigg(\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}\bigg)^{2}v^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\bigg(\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}(1-v^{2})\bigg)-\sigma^{4}=\sigma^{2}\rho^{2},

(7.11)

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}-T^{11}_{M}

\displaystyle=\rho\left(\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}(1-v^{2})-2\sigma^{2}\right)=(1-\sigma^{2})\rho,

(7.12)

\displaystyle T^{01}_{M}

\displaystyle=\rho\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-v^{2}}v=T^{00}_{M}\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1-\sigma^{2}v^{2}}v.

(7.13)

Finally, define the self-similar variable

\displaystyle\xi:=\frac{r}{t},

the metric variable

\displaystyle D:=\sqrt{AB},

(7.14)

and the rescaled density and velocity variables:

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle:=\kappa T^{00}_{M}r^{2},

(7.15)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle:=\frac{\kappa T^{01}_{M}r^{2}}{\xi z},

(7.16)

respectively. By (
7.7
) and (
7.8
), we have

\displaystyle w=\frac{\Xi}{\xi},

(7.17)

where

\displaystyle\Xi=\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}v,

so
\Xi=v
when
\sigma=0
, the case we restrict to below. Assuming the mapping
(t,r)\to(t,\xi)
is regular, the following theorem gives four equations in independent variables
(t,\xi)
which are equivalent
21
21

21

By equivalent, we mean equivalent for smooth solutions under regular transformations of the independent and dependent variables, and where constraints are satisfied subject to appropriate initial boundary data, see
[
12
]
.
to (
7.1
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
) and (
7.5
).

Theorem 32
.

Assume equation of state (
7.6
). Then equations (
7.1
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
), and (
7.5
) are equivalent to the following four equations in unknowns
A(t,\xi)
,
D(t,\xi)
,
z(t,\xi)
and
w(t,\xi)
:

\displaystyle\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-z+(1-A),

(7.18)

\displaystyle\xi D_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{2A}\bigg(2(1-A)-(1-\sigma^{2})\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z\bigg),

(7.19)

\displaystyle tz_{t}+\xi\big((-1+Dw)z\big)_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-Dwz,

(7.20)

\displaystyle tw_{t}+(-1+Dw)\xi w_{\xi}

\displaystyle-w+Dw^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\xi z}\bigg(D\Xi^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}z\bigg)_{\xi}

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma^{2}\xi}{z}\bigg(D\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\frac{z}{\xi^{2}}\bigg)_{\xi}=\text{RHS},

(7.21)

where

\displaystyle\text{RHS}=-\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\frac{D}{2A}\bigg((1-\sigma^{2})(1-A)+2\sigma^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z\bigg).

Moreover, equations (
7.18
)–(
7.21
) imply the two equations:

\displaystyle tA_{t}+\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=wz,

(7.22)

\displaystyle\xi\frac{B_{\xi}}{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{A}\frac{\sigma^{2}+v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z+\frac{1-A}{A},

(7.23)

which are equivalent to (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) respectively.

We call system (
7.18
)–(
7.21
) the
self-similar Einstein field equations
. Note again that (
7.18
)–(
7.21
) are not the Einstein field equations in
(t,\xi)
coordinates, but rather the Einstein field equations in SSC
(t,r)
, expressed in terms of variables
(t,\xi)
. That is,
A
,
B
and
v
are the metric components and invariant velocity in SSC
(t,r)
coordinates, not
(t,\xi)
coordinates.

The case
\sigma=0
of Theorem
32
, which is the basis for this paper, is stated in the following Corollary.

Corollary 33
.

Assume equation of state (
7.6
) with
\sigma=0
(
p=0
). Then equations (
7.1
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) are equivalent to the following four equations in unknowns
A(t,\xi)
,
D(t,\xi)
,
z(t,\xi)
and
w(t,\xi)
:

\displaystyle\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-z+(1-A),

(7.24)

\displaystyle\xi D_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{2A}\big(2(1-A)-(1-\xi^{2}w^{2})z\big),

(7.25)

\displaystyle tz_{t}+\xi\big((-1+Dw)z\big)_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-Dwz,

(7.26)

\displaystyle tw_{t}+\xi(-1+Dw)w_{\xi}

\displaystyle=w-D\bigg(w^{2}+\frac{1}{2\xi^{2}}(1-\xi^{2}w^{2})\frac{1-A}{A}\bigg).

(7.27)

Moreover, equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) imply the two equations:

\displaystyle tA_{t}+\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=wz,

(7.28)

\displaystyle\xi\frac{B_{\xi}}{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi^{2}w^{2}}{A}z+\frac{1-A}{A},

(7.29)

which are equivalent to (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) respectively.

The proof of Theorem
32
is given in Section
13.3
.

The next theorem justifies our proposal that the ambient Euclidean coordinate system
\vec{x}=(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=(t,x,y,z)
associated with our spherical SSC system
(t,r)
provides the coordinate system that imposes the correct smoothness condition for SSC solutions in a neighborhood of
r=0
.

Theorem 34
.

Assume
A(t,\xi)
,
D(t,\xi)
,
z(t,\xi)
and
w(t,\xi)
are a given smooth solution of the
p=0
equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) satisfying:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=1+O(\xi^{2}),

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=1+O(\xi^{2}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=O(\xi^{2}),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=w_{0}(t)+O(\xi^{2}),

(7.30)

and assume that at
t=t_{*}>0
the solution agrees with initial data:

\displaystyle A(t_{*},\xi)

\displaystyle=\bar{A}(\xi),

\displaystyle D(t_{*},\xi)

\displaystyle=\bar{D}(\xi),

\displaystyle z(t_{*},\xi)

\displaystyle=\bar{z}(\xi),

\displaystyle w(t_{*},\xi)

\displaystyle=\bar{w}(\xi),

such that each initial data function
\bar{A}(\xi)
,
\bar{D}(\xi)
,
\bar{z}(\xi)
and
\bar{w}(\xi)
satisfies the condition that all odd derivatives vanish at
\xi=0
. Then all odd derivatives of
A(t,0)
,
D(t,0)
,
z(t,0)
and
w(t,0)
vanish for all
t>t_{*}
.

The proof of Theorem
34
is given in Section
13.4
.

8
The STV-ODE

To describe the evolution of solutions of equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) near
\xi=0
in SSCNG, we assume the following asymptotic ansatz:

\displaystyle A(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=1+A_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+A_{4}(t)\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(8.1)

\displaystyle D(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=1+D_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(8.2)

\displaystyle z(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=z_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+z_{4}(t)\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(8.3)

\displaystyle w(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=w_{0}(t)+w_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}).

(8.4)

Note that starting the expansion of
A
and
D
at unity forces
B=1
at
\xi=0
, so this imposes the normalized gauge condition. Note also that we have included only even powers of
\xi
, which is equivalent to the assumption that the solution is smooth at
\xi=r=0
, see
[
29
]
. As a special case, we have from Section
6
the following expansion of the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann solution:

\displaystyle A_{F}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+A_{2}^{F}\xi^{2}+A_{4}^{F}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(8.5)

\displaystyle D_{F}(\xi)

\displaystyle=1+D_{2}^{F}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(8.6)

\displaystyle z_{F}(\xi)

\displaystyle=z_{2}^{F}\xi^{2}+z_{4}^{F}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(8.7)

\displaystyle w_{F}(\xi)

\displaystyle=w_{0}^{F}+w_{2}^{F}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(8.8)

with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{F}:=(z_{2}^{F},w_{0}^{F},z_{4}^{F},w_{2}^{F})=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\bigg)

(8.9)

and:

\displaystyle A_{2}^{F}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2}^{F}=-\frac{4}{9},

(8.10)

\displaystyle A_{4}^{F}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4}^{F}=-\frac{8}{27},

(8.11)

\displaystyle D_{2}^{F}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}^{F}=-\frac{1}{9}.

(8.12)

The time independence of the coefficients of powers of
\xi
in (
8.5
)–(
8.8
) reflects the fact that the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetime is self-similar in SSCNG coordinates
[
30
]
. To see that the equations for the ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) close at every even power of
\xi
, and to obtain the equations, we substitute (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) into equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) and collect like powers of
\xi
. The result up to order
O(\xi^{6})
in
z
and order
O(\xi^{4})
in
w
(which is
O(\xi^{5})
in velocity
v=\xi w
) is stated in the following theorem.

Theorem 35
.

Putting the ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) into equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) and equating like powers of
\xi
leads to the following autonomous ODE for
A_{i}=A_{i}(t)
,
D_{i}=D_{i}(t)
,
z_{i}=z_{i}(t)
and
w_{i}=w_{i}(t)
:

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0},

(8.13)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w_{0}-w_{0}^{2},

(8.14)

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{12}z_{2}^{2}w_{0}-5w_{0}z_{4}+4z_{4}-5z_{2}w_{2},

(8.15)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{24}z_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}z_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}-4w_{0}w_{2}+3w_{2},

(8.16)

together with:

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2},

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4},

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}.

(8.17)

We refer to system (
8.13
)–(
8.16
) as the STV-ODE of order
n=2
.

The proof of Theorem
35
is given in Section
13.5
.

Note that relations (
8.10
) and (
8.12
) between metric components and fluid variables at each order in the expansion of the
k=0
Friedmann solution anticipates (
8.17
), which holds at each order in the expansion of general smooth solutions in powers of
\xi
in SSCNG coordinates. This simplifies the ODE for the corrections significantly, as it reduces the number of unknowns from seven, to the four unknowns
(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
.

9
The Phase Portrait for The STV-ODE

Recall that letting
\tau=\ln t
gives
22
22

22

We use a dot to denote
\frac{d}{dt}
and a prime to denote
\frac{d}{d\tau}
.

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}=t\frac{d}{dt},

and this turns the ODE (
8.13
)–(
8.16
) into an autonomous system in
\tau
. Letting

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{4}),

system (
8.13
)–(
8.16
) takes the form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{U}=\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U}),

where

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\boldsymbol{U}=t\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\dot{z}_{2}\\
\dot{w}_{0}\\
\dot{z}_{4}\\
\dot{w}_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0}\\
-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w_{0}-w_{0}^{2}\\
\frac{5}{12}w_{0}z_{2}^{2}+4z_{4}-5w_{0}z_{4}-5z_{2}w_{2}\\
-\frac{1}{24}z_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}z_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+3w_{2}-4w_{0}w_{2}\end{array}\right)=:\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U}).

Since (
9
) is autonomous, it can be described by a phase portrait, and the phase portrait is essentially determined by the structure of its rest points. Writing the ODE in the order
(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
and solving
\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U})=0
for
\boldsymbol{U}
gives the rest points:

\displaystyle SM

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{U}_{F}=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\bigg),

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=(0,1,0,0),

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,0).

(9.9)

Note that the first two equations in (
9
) close in variables
(z_{2},w_{0})
to form the
2\times 2
system

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{2}\\
w_{0}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0}\\
-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w_{0}-w_{0}^{2}\end{array}\right)=:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f(z_{2},w_{0})\\
g(z_{2},w_{0})\end{array}\right)=:\boldsymbol{f}(z_{2},w_{0}),

and the rest points of (
9
) are the restriction of the rest points
SM
,
M
and
U
of the
4\times 4
system to the first two components. Regarding the rest point
SM
, we know from Section
6
that the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann metric (the Standard Model) is self-similar in SSCNG, so we know ahead of time that its expansion in powers of
\xi
in (
8.5
)–(
8.8
) implies
\boldsymbol{U}_{F}
must determine a rest point of system (
9
), with this rest point being
SM
. Thus
SM
is the solution of (
9
) corresponding to the first two terms in the expansion of the Standard Model in even powers of
\xi
, with the evolution of perturbations of
SM
described by nearby solutions of (
9
). As for the rest point
M
, we observe that
z=0
and
w=1
(
v=\xi
) solves the self-similar Einstein field equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) exactly with
A=B=D=1
, so a rest point
M
satisfying
w_{0}=1
with all other coefficients being null must also be a rest point at every level of expansion of solutions of the self-similar Einstein field equations in even powers of
\xi
. We will see at the level of (
9
) and (
9
) that
SM
is an unstable saddle rest point and
M
is a stable rest point, although
M
is also a stable rest point for all higher levels of approximation too. Moreover, the underdense side of the unstable manifold of
SM
contains trajectories which connect
SM
to
M
, one of which, together with its time translations
\tau\to\tau-\Delta_{0}
, corresponds to the one parameter family of
k<0
Friedmann spacetimes. In Theorem
44
, we prove that all trajectories that tend to
M
in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane also tend to
M
at every order of expansion of smooth solutions of (
7.24
)–(
7.27
). In the next section we begin the description of the phase portrait of the
2\times 2
system (
9
). This will play a basic role in the description of the phase portrait for (
9
), which will be discussed in the section after. Without confusion, we use
SM
,
M
and
U
to label the three rest points in the
2\times 2
,
4\times 4
and general
2n\times 2n
phase portraits.

9.1
Phase Portrait for the
2\times 2
System

In this section our notation is to use
\boldsymbol{u}=(z_{2},w_{0})
,
\boldsymbol{v}=(z_{4},w_{2})
and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}=(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2}).

Now equations (
8.13
)–(
8.16
) close at each even order of
\xi
, so to begin, we describe the phase portrait for the two equations (
8.13
) and (
8.14
) in
\boldsymbol{u}=(z_{2},w_{0})
, given by (
9
). Solutions of
2\times 2
autonomous ODE are characterized by their phase portrait. For this, observe first that

\displaystyle f(z_{2},w_{0})=2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0}=0

gives the
z_{2}
contour as
w_{0}=\frac{2}{3}
or
z_{2}=0
, and

\displaystyle g(z_{2},w_{0})=-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w_{0}-w_{0}^{2}=0

gives the
w_{0}
contour as
z_{2}=6w_{0}(1-w_{0})
. Thus in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane, the contours intersect in the three rest points of the system:

\displaystyle SM

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3}\bigg),

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=(0,1),

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=(0,0).

Here
(z_{2}^{F},w_{0}^{F})=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3})
are the first two components of
\boldsymbol{U}_{F}
, so for notational convenience (and to be consistent with
[
29
]
), we denote by
SM
the rest point corresponding to
\boldsymbol{U}_{F}
in both the
2\times 2
and
4\times 4
systems (
9
) and (
9
) respectively. We now show that
SM
(for Standard Model) is an unstable saddle rest point,
M
(for Minkowski) is a stable rest point and
U
is a fully unstable rest point.

The Jacobian,
d\boldsymbol{f}
, of
\boldsymbol{f}(z_{2},w_{0})
is given by

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{f}(z_{2},w_{0})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2-3w_{0}&-3z_{2}\\
-\frac{1}{6}&1-2w_{0}\end{array}\right).

At the rest point
SM
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{f}\left(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-4\\
-\frac{1}{6}&-\frac{1}{3}\end{array}\right)

and the eigenpairs are given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{A1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}9\\
-\frac{3}{2}\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B1}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{B1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}4\\
1\end{array}\right).

At the rest point
M
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{f}(0,1)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&-1\end{array}\right).

The rest point
M
has the double eigenvalue
\lambda_{M}=-1
, which has a resonant Jordan normal form with a one-dimensional eigenspace:

\displaystyle\lambda_{M}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{M}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right).

Finally, at the rest point
U
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{f}(0,0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&1\end{array}\right)

and the eigenpairs are given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{U1}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{U2}

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-6\\
1\end{array}\right).

The phase portrait for system (
9
) is depicted in Figure
1
. The main feature is that the rest point
SM
, which corresponds to the Standard Model
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetime, is an unstable saddle rest point. Note first that
z_{2}=0
is a solution trajectory of (
9
), so
z_{2}\geq 0
is an invariant region, since solution trajectories never cross in autonomous systems. Thus the solutions in the stable manifold of
M
consist of all trajectories to the left of the two trajectories in the stable manifold of
SM
in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane, and to the right of
z_{2}=0
. These include all smooth radial underdense perturbations of
SM
, and all of these solutions enter
M
asymptotically from below, along the eigendirection
\boldsymbol{R}_{M}=(0,1)^{T}
, that is, parallel to the
w_{0}
-axis. The unstable manifold of
SM
thus has two components: The trajectories that connect the rest point
SM
to the stable rest point
M
on the underdense (smaller
z_{2}
) side of
SM
, and the trajectories on the overdense (larger
z_{2}
) side of
SM
, which continue to larger values of
z
until they hit
w_{0}=0
. We show in the next section that these two components of the unstable manifold of
SM
correspond to the
p=0
,
k\neq 0
Friedmann spacetimes to leading order in
\xi
. To complete the picture, the stable manifold of
SM
on the underdense side is a single trajectory connecting the rest point
U
to
SM
, and the stable manifold of
SM
on the overdense side is a trajectory which goes off to infinity.

9.2
The
2\times 2
Unstable Manifold of
SM
is
k=-1
Friedmann

The
k=-1
Friedmann family of solutions is parameterized by
\Delta_{0}>0
, and each one solves the STV-PDE exactly. It follows that the leading order term in the expansion of the
k=-1
Friedmann solutions, that is, the projection of this family of solutions onto the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane, will produce a family of exact solutions
(z_{2}(t),w_{0}(t))
of system (
9
), also parameterized by
\Delta_{0}
. The next theorem gives an implicit expression for these solutions. From this expression we find that each
k=-1
Friedmann solution moves from the rest point
SM
to the rest point
M
as
\tau
ranges from
-\infty
to
+\infty
. It follows that this solution provides an exact expression for the portion of the unstable manifold of
SM
consisting of the trajectory which connects
SM
to
M
, and the parameter
\Delta_{0}>0
represents time translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{0}
with
\tau_{0}=\Delta_{0}
. In this way the
k=-1
Friedmann family provides an exact formula for the portion of the unstable manifold of
SM
given by the trajectory which connects
SM
to
M
in the phase portrait of the
2\times 2
system (
9
).

Theorem 36
.

The expansion of the
k=-1
Friedmann solution (with parameter
\Delta_{0}>0
) in even powers of
\xi
produces the following implicit formulas for
z_{2}(t)
and
w_{0}(t)
in terms of
\theta=\theta(t)
:

\displaystyle z_{2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{z}_{2}(\theta)=\frac{6(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{2}}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{3}}=-3A_{2},

(9.16)

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}_{0}(\theta)=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)\sinh 2\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{2}},

(9.17)

where
\theta\geq 0
is defined as a function of
t\geq 0
through the relation

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta).

(9.18)

Moreover, equation (
9.18
) inverts to define the inverse function

\displaystyle\Theta:(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\to(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=\Theta\Big(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\Big),

and in terms of
\Theta
defined by (
10.23
), the
p=0
,
k=-1
expansion of Friedmann for general
\Delta_{0}>0
is given by:

\displaystyle z_{2}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{z}_{2}\bigg(\Theta\Big(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\Big)\bigg),

(9.19)

\displaystyle w_{0}^{F}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}_{0}\bigg(\Theta\Big(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\Big)\bigg).

(9.20)

Furthermore, for every
\Delta_{0}>0
we have:

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\big(z_{2}^{F}(t),w_{0}^{F}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=SM=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3}\bigg),

(9.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\big(z_{2}^{F}(t),w_{0}^{F}(t)\big)

\displaystyle=M=(0,1).

(9.22)

Note that by (
9.16
) and (
9.17
),
\big(\tilde{z}_{2}(\Theta(t)),\tilde{w}_{0}(\Theta(t))\big)
represents the
k=-1
Friedmann solution in the case
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
.

Proof.

That (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) is an exact solution of system (
9
) follows directly from the fact that it agrees with the leading order expansion of an exact solution of the STV-PDE, namely, the
k=-1
Friedmann solution. Also, that (
9.16
) and (
9.17
) describe the connecting orbit which takes
SM
to
M
follows from (
9.21
) and (
9.22
) by a simple calculation. Theorem
36
is a special case of Theorem
42
, whose proof is given in Sections
13.6
and
13.7
.
∎

In summary, the phase portrait for system (
9
) consists of three rest points:
U
,
SM
and
M
, and the connecting orbit between
SM
and
M
is the projection of the
k=-1
Friedmann solution onto the leading order
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane, with this trajectory described exactly by (
9.19
) and (
9.20
). The region
z_{2}\geq 0
is an invariant region because
z_{2}=0
solves (
9
) and solution trajectories never cross in autonomous systems. The stable manifold of
M
consists of all trajectories to the left of the two trajectories in the stable manifold of
SM
in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane and to the right of
z_{2}=0
. All of these solutions, including
(z_{2}^{F}(t),w_{0}^{F}(t))
, enter
M
asymptotically, from below, along the eigendirection parallel to the
w_{0}
-axis. We now quantify this decay to
M
with estimates, and prove that, under appropriate time translation, all solutions entering
M
converge to
k=-1
Friedmann at a faster rate than they converge to
M
for each fixed
r
(but not fixed
\xi
) as
t\to\infty
.

To study decay to
M=(0,1)
, let
x=z_{2}
,
y=1-w_{0}
and write system (
9
) in the equivalent form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}-x+3xy\\
\frac{1}{6}x-y+y^{2}\end{array}\right)=\boldsymbol{f}(x,y).

Separating the linear and nonlinear parts, we obtain the equivalent system in matrix form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
\frac{1}{6}&-1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right)+y\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}3&0\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right).

Note that the first matrix is
d\boldsymbol{f}(0,1)
, where
(x,y)=(0,0)
is a non-degenerate rest point representing
M
in the
(x,y)
-plane, so this rest point has the double eigenvalue
\lambda_{M}=-1
and a resonant Jordan normal form with single eigenvector
\boldsymbol{R}_{M}=(0,1)^{T}
. By the Hartman–Grobman theorem, solutions of nonlinear autonomous systems entering a non-degenerate rest point, look asymptotically, to within quadratic errors
|\boldsymbol{U}|^{2}=|(x,y)|^{2}
, like the corresponding solution of the linear system.

Linearizing around the rest point
U=(0,0)
of (
9.2
), we obtain the linear system

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
\frac{1}{6}&-1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x\\
y\end{array}\right).

The solution
(\bar{x},\bar{y})
of the linear system (
9.2
), satisfying initial data:

\displaystyle\bar{x}(\tau_{*})

\displaystyle=x_{*},

\displaystyle\bar{y}(\tau_{*})

\displaystyle=y_{*},

(9.39)

is

\displaystyle\bar{x}(\tau)

\displaystyle=x_{*}e^{-\tau},

\displaystyle\bar{y}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\bigg(e^{\tau_{*}}y_{*}+\frac{1}{6}x_{*}(\tau-\tau_{*})\bigg)e^{-\tau}.

(9.40)

Thus, assuming without loss of generality that
\tau_{*}\geq 1
, by the Hartman–Grobman theorem, we can initially conclude that the solution
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(x(\tau),y(\tau))
of the nonlinear system (
9.2
) satisfying the same initial condition (
9.39
), is given by:

\displaystyle x(\tau)

\displaystyle=a(\tau)e^{-\tau},

(9.41)

\displaystyle y(\tau)

\displaystyle=b(\tau)\tau e^{-\tau},

(9.42)

where
a(\tau)
and
b(\tau)
are uniformly bounded, that is,

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C,

\displaystyle|b(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C,

(9.43)

for some constant
C
depending only on
x_{*}
and
y_{*}
. That is, a smooth trajectory is bounded on any compact interval
[\tau_{*},\tau]
, so the uniformity of
C
over all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
follows from the decay to
M
as
\tau\to\infty
. The assumption
\tau_{*}\geq 1
, which is no loss of generality in light of the time translation invariance of autonomous systems of ODE, simplifies formulas by allowing us to bound constants by
\tau
. To obtain a more refined estimate, we use the Hartman–Grobman theorem, which implies that a solution
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(x(\tau),y(\tau))
of the nonlinear system (
9.2
) satisfying the same initial condition (
9.39
), satisfies

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}=\bar{\boldsymbol{U}}+O\big(|\boldsymbol{U}|^{2}\big).

This, together with (
9.41
) and (
9.42
), translates into:

\displaystyle x(\tau)

\displaystyle=x_{*}e^{-\tau}+a^{2}(\tau)e^{-2\tau},

(9.44)

\displaystyle y(\tau)

\displaystyle=\bigg(e^{\tau_{*}}y_{*}+\frac{1}{6}x_{*}(\tau-\tau_{*})\bigg)e^{-\tau}+b^{2}(\tau)e^{-2\tau}.

(9.45)

Thus
y(\tau)
decays to
M
by a factor of
\tau
slower than
x(\tau)
due to the resonant double eigenvalue of the rest point
M
. The next lemma shows that the difference between two solutions, under appropriate time translation, decays faster. We use this to establish that, at leading order, solutions tending to
M
decay to the
k=-1
Friedmann solutions by a factor of
\tau
faster than they decay to
M
.

Lemma 37
.

Let
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}(\tau)=(x_{1}(\tau),y_{1}(\tau))
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}(\tau)=(x_{2}(\tau),y_{2}(\tau))
be two solutions of (
9.2
) satisfying (
9.41
)–(
9.43
) with initial conditions:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{1}(\tau_{*})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{*}=(x_{1}^{*},y_{1}^{*}),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{2}(\tau_{*})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{*}=(x_{2}^{*},y_{2}^{*}),

respectively, where

\displaystyle x_{1}^{*}=x_{2}^{*}=x^{*}

and, without loss of generality, assume
\tau_{*}\geq 1
. Then there exists a constant
C
, depending only on
x^{*}
,
y_{1}^{*}
,
y_{2}^{*}
and
\tau_{*}
, such that

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{U}_{1}-\boldsymbol{U}_{2}|\leq Ce^{-\tau}

(9.46)

for all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}\geq 1
.

That is, by (
9.46
), both
|x_{2}(\tau)-x_{1}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{-\tau}
and
|y_{2}(\tau)-y_{1}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{-\tau}
, so comparing (
9.46
) to (
9.45
), the two solutions converge to each other faster than they decay separately to the rest point
M
.

Proof.

By (
9.44
),

\displaystyle|x_{2}(\tau)-x_{1}(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq\big(a_{2}(\tau)^{2}+a_{1}(\tau)^{2}\big)e^{-2\tau}\leq C_{x}e^{-1},

\displaystyle|y_{2}(\tau)-y_{1}(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq\big(e^{\tau_{*}}(|y_{1}^{*}|+|y_{2}^{*}|)\big)e^{-t}+\big(b_{1}(\tau)^{2}+b_{2}(\tau)^{2}\big)e^{-2\tau}\leq C_{y}e^{-1},

for
C=\max\{C_{x},C_{y}\}
, where by (
9.43
),

\displaystyle C_{x}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{1}^{2},

\displaystyle C_{y}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\tau_{*}}(|y_{1}^{*}|+|y_{2}^{*}|)+C_{1}^{2}.

∎

We use this Lemma to prove the following theorem, which implies that, for any given leading order solution
(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))
which tends to the rest point
M
as
\tau\to\infty
, there always exists a value of
\delta>0
such that it decays faster asymptotically to the
k=-1
Friedmann spacetime
(z_{2}^{F}(\tau),w_{0}^{F}(\tau))
with
\Delta_{0}=\delta
, than it decays to the rest point
M
. Again, since smooth solutions of (
9.2
) starting at
\tau_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
are bounded on the compact interval
[\tau_{*},\tau]
for any
\tau_{*}\leq\tau<\infty
, and solutions are preserved under time translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{*}+1
because system (
9.2
) is autonomous, then to keep things simple, and without loss of generality, we state the following theorem in terms of solutions defined for
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
, assuming initial time
\tau_{*}\geq 1
.

Theorem 38
.

Let
\tau_{*}\geq 1
and
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))
be any solution of the
2\times 2
system (
9
), with initial condition
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau_{*})=\boldsymbol{U}_{*}=(z_{2}^{*},w_{0}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
, which tends to the rest point
M=(0,1)
as
\tau\to\infty
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the initial data
\boldsymbol{U}_{*}
and
\tau_{*}
, such that:

\displaystyle z_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=a(\tau)e^{-\tau},

(9.47)

\displaystyle w_{0}(\tau)

\displaystyle=b(\tau)\tau e^{-\tau},

(9.48)

where

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C,

\displaystyle|b(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C.

(9.49)

Moreover, there exists a
k<-1
Friedmann solution with leading order components
(z_{2}^{F}(\tau),w_{0}^{F}(\tau))
and a constant
C_{F}>0
, depending only on the initial data
\boldsymbol{U}_{*}
and
\tau_{*}
, such that:

\displaystyle\big|z_{2}(\tau)-z_{2}^{F}(\tau)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{F}e^{-\tau},

(9.50)

\displaystyle\big|w_{0}(\tau)-w_{0}^{F}(\tau)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{F}e^{-\tau},

(9.51)

or in terms of
t=e^{\tau}
,

\displaystyle\big|z_{2}(\ln t)-z_{2}^{F}(\ln t)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{F}}{t},

(9.52)

\displaystyle\big|w_{0}(\ln t)-w_{0}^{F}(\ln t)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{F}}{t}.

(9.53)

Proof.

Equations (
9.47
), (
9.48
) and (
9.50
) follow directly from (
9.41
)–(
9.43
). To apply Lemma
37
and obtain an improvement of factor
\tau
over (
9.48
), note that since
z_{2}^{*}>0
, it follows that
z_{2}(\tau)
takes on all values in the interval
(0,z_{2}^{*})
as
\tau
ranges from
\tau_{0}
to
\infty
. Thus, with the possible change of a constant, we can assume without loss of generality that
\tau_{*}\in(0,\frac{4}{3})
. Since
\tilde{z}_{2}
, defined in (
9.16
), that is, the leading order
k=-1
Friedmann solution with
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
, ranges from the
SM
value
z_{2}=\frac{4}{3}
to the
M
value
z_{2}=0
as
\tau
ranges from
-\infty
to
\infty
, it follows that there must exist a time
\tau_{F}
at which
\tilde{z}_{2}(\tau_{F})=z_{2}(\tau_{*})=z_{2}^{*}
. Then setting
\Delta_{0}=\tau_{F}-\tau_{*}
, we have

\displaystyle z_{2}^{F}(\tau)=\tilde{z}_{2}(\tau-\Delta_{0}).

Therefore Lemma
37
applies, and the improved rate (
9.51
) follows from (
9.46
).
∎

We have proven that the coefficients
(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))
of solutions which tend to the rest point
M
, such as the
k<0
Friedmann solutions, actually converge to a
k<0
Friedmann solution by a factor
\tau
faster than they decay to
M
. Thus at the level of the
2\times 2
system, the
k<0
family of spacetimes is a forward time global attractor for nearby solutions. Although these solutions which enter
M
are stable in forward direction, all of them, including
k<0
Friedmann solutions, are unstable in backward time. Thus one expects to see
k<0
Friedmann solutions at late times, but we cannot assume solutions close to
k<0
Friedmann at late times were also close to
k<0
Friedmann solutions at early times. In the next section we show that for the
4\times 4
system (
9
), the
k<0
Friedmann solutions represent just a single parameter in a two parameter family of solutions which span the unstable manifold of
SM
at this order. This extra parameter induces third order effects in the velocity, which differentiate general perturbations of
SM
that tend to
M
as
t\to\infty
from the
k=-1
Friedmann spacetimes.
23
23

23

For example, the third order velocity term affects the third order correction to redshift vs luminosity, the term which differentiates the
k<0
Friedmann spacetimes from the
k=0
Friedmann spacetime with a cosmological constant, see
[
29
]
.

9.3
Phase Portrait for the
4\times 4
System

We now describe the phase portrait for the
4\times 4
system (
9
). We first analyze the rest points
SM
,
M
and
U
given in (
9.9
). Again observe that the rest points in the
2\times 2
system are the restriction of the rest points
SM
,
M
and
U
to the
\boldsymbol{U}=(z_{2},w_{0})
plane, and thus we label them the same. The nonlinear function on the right hand side of (
9
) is

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U})=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0}\\
-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w_{0}-w_{0}^{2}\\
\frac{5}{12}w_{0}z_{2}^{2}+4z_{4}-5w_{0}z_{4}-5z_{2}w_{2}\\
-\frac{1}{24}z_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}z_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+3w_{2}-4w_{0}w_{2}\end{array}\right)

and the Jacobian of
\boldsymbol{F}
at
\boldsymbol{U}=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U})=\left(\begin{array}[]{llll}-3w_{0}+2&-3z_{2}&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&1-2w_{0}&0&0\\
\frac{5}{6}z_{2}w_{0}-5w_{2}&\frac{5}{12}z_{2}^{2}-5z_{4}&4-5w_{0}&-5z_{2}\\
-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}+\frac{1}{4}w_{0}^{2}&\frac{1}{2}z_{2}w_{0}-4w_{2}&-\frac{1}{10}&3-4w_{0}\end{array}\right).

For the rest point
SM
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{F}\left(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&-4&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&-\frac{1}{3}&0&0\\
-\frac{10}{27}&-\frac{20}{3}&\frac{2}{3}&-\frac{20}{3}\\
0&-\frac{4}{9}&-\frac{1}{10}&\frac{1}{3}\end{array}\right)

and the eigenpairs are given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{A1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}9\\
-\frac{3}{2}\\
\frac{10}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B1}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{B1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}4\\
1\\
\frac{80}{9}\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{A2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
-10\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{B2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
\frac{20}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right).

For the rest point
M
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{F}(0,1,0,0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}-1&0&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&-1&0&0\\
0&0&-1&0\\
\frac{1}{4}&0&-\frac{1}{10}&-1\end{array}\right)

and has a single repeated eigenvalue
\lambda=-1
, which has the two-dimensional eigenspace:

\displaystyle\lambda_{M}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{M1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{M}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{M2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{array}\right).

Finally, for the rest point
U
, the Jacobian is

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{F}(0,0,0,0)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}2&0&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&1&0&0\\
0&0&4&0\\
0&0&-\frac{1}{10}&3\end{array}\right)

and the eigenpairs are given by:

\displaystyle\lambda_{U1}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{U2}

\displaystyle=2,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-6\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{U3}

\displaystyle=3,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U3}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{U4}

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{U4}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
-10\\
1\end{array}\right).

For future use, we record the system in
(z_{4},w_{2})
one obtains by substituting the
SM
values for
(z_{2},w_{0})
into the
4\times 4
system (
9
), namely,
(z_{2},w_{0})=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3})
. The result is the following
2\times 2
non-autonomous system in
(z_{4},w_{2})

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}z_{4}\\
w_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}F_{3}(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},z_{4},w_{2})\\
F_{4}(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},z_{4},w_{2})\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{2}{3}z_{4}-\frac{20}{3}w_{2}+\frac{40}{81}\\
-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+w_{2}+\frac{2}{27}\end{array}\right).

We now prove that any trajectory whose restriction to the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane enters the stable rest point
M=(0,1)
in forward time, must enter the stable rest point
M=(0,1,0,0)
in the four-dimensional phase portrait
\boldsymbol{U}=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
as well. Since smooth solutions of (
9
) starting at
\tau=\tau_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\tau_{*}\leq 1
are bounded on the compact interval
[\tau_{*},1]
, and being autonomous, solutions are preserved under time translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{*}+1
, then to keep things simple, and without loss of generality, we state the following theorem in terms of solutions defined on the interval
1\leq\tau\leq\infty
.

Theorem 39
.

Assume
(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau),z_{4}(\tau),w_{2}(\tau))
is a solution of the initial value problem for the
4\times 4
system (
9
) with initial data

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}(\tau_{*})=\boldsymbol{U}_{*}.

In addition, assume, without loss of generality, that
\tau_{*}=1
and that the first two components (which solve (
9
)) tend to the rest point
M
in positive time, that is, assume

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))=(0,1).

Then

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau),z_{4}(\tau),w_{2}(\tau))=(0,1,0,0)=M

(9.112)

and there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the system (
9
) and initial data
\boldsymbol{U}_{*}
, such that

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)-M\right|\leq C\tau e^{-\tau}

(9.113)

for all
\tau\geq 1
. Furthermore, there exists a
\Delta_{0}>0
and
C>0
, depending only on the system (
9
) and initial data
\boldsymbol{U}_{*}
, such that the associated
k<0
Friedmann solution

\boldsymbol{U}^{F}(\tau)=(z_{2}^{F}(\tau),w_{0}^{F}(\tau),z_{4}^{F}(\tau),w_{2}^{F}(\tau))

with parameter
\Delta_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle\big|(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))-(z_{2}^{F}(\tau),w_{0}^{F}(\tau))\big|\leq Ce^{-\tau}

(9.114)

and

\displaystyle\big|\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{U}^{F}(\tau)\big|\leq C\tau e^{-\tau},

(9.115)

for all
\tau\geq 1
.

Proof.

This is the special
n=2
case of the the more general theorem, Theorem
45
, given in Section
11
.
∎

We now determine the possible backward time asymptotics of solutions whose projection in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane is the unstable manifold of
SM=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3})
, which takes
SM
to
M=(0,1)
.

Theorem 40
.

Assume
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau),z_{4}(\tau),w_{2}(\tau))
is a solution of the
4\times 4
system (
9
) such that

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3}\bigg)=SM

Then there are two cases:

(i)

\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)
is not in the unstable manifold of
SM
.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)
is a trajectory in the unstable manifold of
SM
, in which case

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=SM.

In Case (i), the solutions
(z_{4}(\tau),w_{2}(\tau))
tend in backward time to the stable manifold of the
2\times 2
linear system

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{2}{3}&-\frac{20}{3}\\
-\frac{1}{10}&\frac{1}{3}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\right),

where
u=z_{4}-\frac{40}{27}
,
v=w_{2}-\frac{2}{9}
and the stable manifold of system (
40
) is the line

\displaystyle z_{4}-\frac{40}{27}=\frac{20}{3}\Big(w_{2}-\frac{2}{9}\Big).

(9.122)

Moreover, solutions of (
40
) are exact solutions of the fully nonlinear system (
9
), allowing for arbitrary initial conditions
(u_{*},v_{*})=(u(t_{*}),v(t_{*}))
.

In Case (ii), for each
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)
there exists a unique value of
(a,b)
such that in the limit
\tau\to-\infty
,
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)
becomes asymptotic to the linearized solution

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)\sim ae^{\frac{2}{3}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+be^{\frac{4}{3}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

(9.123)

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}9\\
-\frac{3}{2}\\
\frac{10}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
-10\\
1\end{array}\right).

It follows that all trajectories in the unstable manifold of
SM
satisfying
a\neq 0
enter
SM
in backward time along
\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}
, the eigendirection of the dominant eigenvalue.

Proof.

In Case (i), since

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3}\bigg),

the solution components
(z_{4}(\tau),w_{2}(\tau))
tend asymptotically to a solution of the
2\times 2
system obtained by substituting the constant values
(z_{2},w_{0})=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3})
into (
9
). The result is the linear homogeneous constant coefficient
2\times 2
system in
(z_{4},w_{2})
, given by:

\displaystyle z_{4}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}z_{4}-\frac{20}{3}w_{2}+\frac{40}{81},

(9.132)

\displaystyle w_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+\frac{1}{3}w_{2}+\frac{2}{27},

(9.133)

which is equivalent to (
40
) upon setting
u=z_{4}-\frac{40}{27}
and
v=w_{2}-\frac{2}{9}
. Clearly the
SM
values
z_{4}=\frac{40}{27}
and
w_{2}=\frac{2}{9}
give the unique rest point of system (
9.132
)–(
9.133
). Moreover, since
(z_{2}(\tau),w_{0}(\tau))=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3})
solve system (
9
) exactly, it follows that solutions of (
9.132
)–(
9.133
) are exact solutions of the full
4\times 4
system (
9
). The eigenpairs of system (
40
) are

\displaystyle\lambda_{A2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}^{*}_{A2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-10\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}^{*}_{B2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{20}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right);

consistent with the
4\times 4
eigenpairs (
9.3
). From this we conclude that the backward time asymptotics of solutions in Case (i) are given by the stable manifold of system (
40
), that is, the line (
9.122
). This completes the proof of Case (i).

Consider now Case (ii), the case when the
4\times 4
solution lies in the unstable manifold of the rest point
SM=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})=(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9})
. The eigenpairs of
SM
are:

\displaystyle\lambda_{A1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}9\\
-\frac{3}{2}\\
\frac{10}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B1}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{B1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}4\\
1\\
\frac{80}{9}\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{A2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
-10\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{B2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{B2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
\frac{20}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right).

Thus the positive eigenvalues
\lambda_{A1}=\frac{2}{3}
and
\lambda_{A2}=\frac{4}{3}
give the unstable directions
\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}
and
\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}
respectively. Now solutions of a nonlinear autonomous system are characterized by the solutions of the linearized system in a neighborhood of a rest point, and the linearized solutions which span the unstable manifold of
SM
are

\displaystyle U(\tau)=ae^{\lambda_{A1}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+be^{\lambda_{A2}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}.

This establishes the backward time asymptotics in Case (ii) and thus completes the proof.
∎

Since
SM
is an unstable saddle rest point, we conclude that any perturbation of
SM
which tends to the stable rest point
M
, tends to
M
asymptotically along the unstable manifold of
SM
. Characterizing the unstable manifold of
SM
characterizes the perturbations of
SM
, which give rise to underdense Friedmann-like solutions. This is the topic of the next section.

10
The Unstable Manifold

Recall that the rest point
SM
of the autonomous system (
9
) corresponds to the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetime in the sense that it gives the first two terms of its expansion in even powers of
\xi
in SSCNG. Also recall that
SM
is represented as a rest point in system (
9
) because the
k=0
Friedmann solution is self-similar in SSCNG coordinates, that is, the solution depends only on
\xi
. By (
9.3
)–(
9.3
),
SM
is an unstable saddle rest point with two negative and two positive eigenvalues. The main point, then, is that the
k<0
Friedmann solutions represent only one parameter in a two-parameter family of solutions which lie in the unstable manifold of rest point
SM
in the
4\times 4
system (
9
).
24
24

24

For example, in terms of an expansion of redshift vs luminosity about the center, this extra parameter plays the same role, at third order, as the extra parameter obtained by introducing a cosmological constant
[
29
]
. This issue will be addressed in detail in a forthcoming paper.
By the Hartman–Grobman theorem, the unstable manifold of
SM
in the nonlinear system (
9
) is parameterized by the asymptotic limits given by the unstable manifold of the system obtained by linearizing (
9
) about
SM
, that is, by the linearized system

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(\boldsymbol{U}-\boldsymbol{U}_{F})=d\boldsymbol{F}\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\bigg)(\boldsymbol{U}-\boldsymbol{U}_{F}),

(10.1)

where

\displaystyle d\boldsymbol{F}\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\bigg)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&-4&0&0\\
-\frac{1}{6}&-\frac{1}{3}&0&0\\
-\frac{10}{27}&-\frac{20}{3}&\frac{2}{3}&-\frac{20}{3}\\
0&-\frac{4}{9}&-\frac{1}{10}&\frac{1}{3}\end{array}\right).

The unstable manifold of
SM
in the nonlinear system (
9
) is therefore a family of solutions of (
9
) parameterized by the two parameters
(a,b)
of the unstable manifold (
9.123
) of (
10.1
), namely,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=ae^{\lambda_{A1}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+be^{\lambda_{A2}\tau}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}.

(10.6)

Assume now that
a\neq 0
, which is equivalent to assuming that the trajectory in the
4\times 4
system (
9
) projects to the unstable manifold in the
2\times 2
system (
9
), and let
\Sigma_{SM}
denote the (essential) subset of the unstable manifold of
SM
in the nonlinear system (
9
) with asymptotic limits at
SM
given by (
10.6
) when
a\neq 0
. Since
a\neq 0
, we can make the translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{0}
and
b\to\beta
to scale
a
to
\pm 1
. The two parameter family
\Sigma_{SM}
is then parameterized by linearized solutions of the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=\pm e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

depending on
(\tau_{0},\beta)
. Here
\pm
determines the side of the unstable manifold of
SM
in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane,
\tau_{0}
represents the time translation freedom of the autonomous system (
9
) and
\beta
names the non-intersecting trajectories of the solutions in
\Sigma_{SM}
. In this section we prove that unique values
\beta=\beta_{F}^{\pm}
determine unique trajectories on each side of the unstable manifold of
SM
which correspond to the
k>0
and
k<0
Friedmann spacetimes respectively, that is, they correspond to the evolution of the first two terms in the expansion of the
k\neq 0
Friedmann solutions in powers of
\xi
in SSCNG coordinates.
25
25

25

We prove in Section
12
that
\beta_{F}^{-}=0
. It is likely also the case that
\beta_{F}^{+}=0
.
More precisely, the
k<0
Friedmann family of solutions is the unique trajectory in
\Sigma_{SM}
corresponding to the linearized trajectory

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{-}(\tau)=-e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta_{F}^{-}e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

and as a solution of the nonlinear system (
9
), the
k<0
trajectory emanates from
SM
on the smaller
z_{2}
(underdense) side of
SM
and tends to
M
as
\tau\to\infty
; the
k>0
Friedmann family of solutions corresponds to the unique trajectory in
\Sigma_{SM}
corresponding to the linearized trajectory

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{+}(\tau-\tau_{0})=e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta_{F}^{+}e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

and as a solution of the nonlinear system (
9
), the
k>0
trajectory emanates from
SM
on the larger
z_{2}
(overdense) side of
SM
and intersects
w_{0}=0
at some finite positive time. Moreover, the one degree of freedom associated with time translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{0}
accounts for the gauge freedom
\Delta_{0}
in
k\neq 0
Friedmann spacetimes through the relation
\tau_{0}=\Delta_{0}+\tau_{F}
for some normalizing constant
\tau_{F}
, see (
5.17
). The fact that the
4\times 4
unstable manifold of
SM
is a two parameter family of solutions containing the one parameter family of Friedmann solutions determined by
k
implies that the unstable manifold of
SM
allows for one extra degree of freedom than that accounted for by the Friedmann family, and by this, perturbations of
SM
produce Friedmann-like solutions which admit one more degree of freedom than Friedmann in the redshift vs luminosity relation. Our results are summarized in the following theorem and the remainder of the section is devoted to the proof of this theorem.

Theorem 41
.

Let
\Sigma_{SM}
denote the subset of the unstable manifold of the rest point
SM
in the
4\times 4
system (
9
) which consists of trajectories which project to the unstable manifold of
SM
in the
2\times 2
nonlinear system (
9
). Then
\Sigma_{SM}
is characterized by its backward time asymptotics given by the two parameter family of solutions of the linearized system (
10.1
),

\displaystyle U(\tau)=\pm e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

(10.7)

parameterized by
(\tau_{0},\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where:

\displaystyle\lambda_{A1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}9\\
-\frac{3}{2}\\
\frac{10}{3}\\
1\end{array}\right);

\displaystyle\lambda_{A2}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
-10\\
1\end{array}\right).

Moreover, the two sides of the unstable manifold of
SM
in the
(z_{2},w_{0})
-plane correspond to the decomposition
\Sigma_{SM}=\Sigma_{SM}^{-}\cup\Sigma_{SM}^{+}
where
\Sigma_{SM}^{-}
corresponds to the linearized trajectories

\displaystyle U(\tau)=-e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}

and
\Sigma_{SM}^{+}
corresponds to the linearized trajectories

\displaystyle U(\tau)=e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+\beta e^{\lambda_{A2}(\tau-\tau_{0})}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}.

Furthermore, trajectories in
\Sigma_{SM}^{-}
leave the rest point
SM
in the (underdense) direction of negative
z_{2}
, trajectories in
\Sigma_{SM}^{+}
leave the rest point
SM
in the (overdense) direction of positive
z_{2}
and all trajectories in
\Sigma_{SM}^{-}
tend to rest point
M
as
\tau\to\infty
.

That the structure of the nonlinear manifold
\Sigma_{SM}=\Sigma_{SM}^{-}\cup\Sigma_{SM}^{+}
is characterized by the linearized system (
10.1
) follows directly from the Hartman–Grobman theorem in light of the hyperbolic nature of rest point
SM
in (
9.3
)–(
9.3
). Moreover, the asymptotic limit
M
for solutions in
\Sigma_{SM}^{-}
follows directly from Theorem
39
in light of the fact that when
a\neq 0
, the projection of solutions in
\Sigma_{SM}^{-}
lie on the unstable manifold of
SM
in the
2\times 2
system (
9
), and this is the starting assumption of Theorem
39
. The proof of Theorem
41
is thus a direct consequence of the following theorem which gives exact formulas for the expansion of the
p=0
,
k\neq 0
Friedmann solutions in even powers of
\xi
in SSCNG coordinates.

Theorem 42
.

The Taylor expansion of the
p=0
,
k=\pm 1
Friedmann solution in even powers of
\xi=\frac{r}{t}
, with coefficient functions of
t
, in SSCNG coordinates
(t,\xi)
takes the form:

\displaystyle z(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=z_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+z_{4}(t)\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(10.16)

\displaystyle w(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=w_{0}(t)+w_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(10.17)

\displaystyle A(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=1+A_{2}(t)\xi^{2}+A_{4}(t)\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}).

(10.18)

In the case
k=-1
,
\Delta_{0}>0
,
(z_{i}(t),w_{i}(t))
are given in terms of
\theta=\theta(t)
by the formulas:

\displaystyle z_{2}=\tilde{z}_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{6(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{2}}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{3}}=-3A_{2},

(10.19)

\displaystyle w_{0}=\tilde{w}_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)\sinh 2\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{2}},

(10.20)

\displaystyle z_{4}=\tilde{z}_{4}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{30(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{4}\cosh^{2}\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{6}}=-5A_{4},

(10.21)

\displaystyle w_{2}=\tilde{w}_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=w_{2}^{B}-w_{2}^{A}

(10.22)

\displaystyle=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{3}\cosh\theta}{2(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{5}\sinh\theta}\Big(\sinh^{2}2\theta+(1-2\sinh^{2}\theta)(\cosh 2\theta-1)\Big),

where
\theta\geq 0
is defined as a function of
t\geq 0
through the relation

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta),

(10.23)

Equation (
10.23
) inverts to define the inverse function

\displaystyle\Theta:(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\to(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\theta(t)

\displaystyle=\Theta\bigg(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\bigg),

and in terms of
\Theta
defined by (
10.23
), the expansion of
p=0
,
k=-1
Friedmann for general
\Delta_{0}>0
is given by:

\displaystyle z_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{z}_{i}\bigg(\Theta\bigg(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\bigg)\bigg),

(10.24)

\displaystyle w_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}_{j}\bigg(\Theta\bigg(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\bigg)\bigg),

(10.25)

\displaystyle A_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{A}_{i}\bigg(\Theta\bigg(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\bigg)\bigg).

(10.26)

In the case
k=+1
,
\Delta_{0}>0
,
(z_{i}(t),w_{i}(t))
are given in terms of
\theta=\theta(t)
by the formulas:

\displaystyle z_{2}=\tilde{z}_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{6(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{2}}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{3}}=-3A_{2},

(10.27)

\displaystyle w_{0}=\tilde{w}_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)\sin 2\theta}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{2}},

(10.28)

\displaystyle z_{4}=\tilde{z}_{4}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{30(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{4}\cos^{2}\theta}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{6}}=-5A_{4},

(10.29)

\displaystyle w_{2}=\tilde{w}_{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{3}\cos\theta}{2(1-\cos 2\theta)^{5}\sin\theta}\Big(\sin^{2}2\theta+(1-2\sin^{2}\theta)(1-\cos 2\theta)\Big),

(10.30)

where
\theta\geq 0
is defined as a function of
t\geq 0
through the relation

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}(2\theta-\sin 2\theta).

(10.31)

Equation (
10.31
) inverts to define the inverse function,

\displaystyle\Theta:(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\to\Big(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\Theta\bigg(\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}\bigg),

and in terms
\Theta
defined by (
10.31
), the expansion of
p=0
,
k=+1
Friedmann for general
\Delta_{0}>0
is again given by (
10.24
)–(
10.26
).

Note that although the formulas for
k=\pm 1
Friedmann spacetimes were given in terms of
\theta
as a function of Friedmann time, in formulas (
10.16
)–(
10.31
)
t
is now SSCNG time, not Friedmann time, as explained in the proof below. Note also that since
\Delta_{0}
is the only free parameter in the Friedmann spacetimes, it follows from (
10.24
)–(
10.26
) that the solutions of (
9
) determined by
p=0
,
k\neq 0
Friedmann solutions of the Einstein field equations are given, for general values of the free parameter
\Delta_{0}>0
, by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{-}(\tau-\Delta_{0})=\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}\big(\Theta\circ\exp(\tau-\Delta_{0})\big),

(10.32)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\pm}(\theta)=\big(\tilde{z}_{2}(\theta),\tilde{w}_{0}(\theta),\tilde{z}_{4}(\theta),\tilde{w}_{2}(\theta)\big),

with the right hand side being defined separately by (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) and (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) in the cases
k=-1
and
k=+1
respectively. Equation (
10.32
) shows that the
k\pm 1
Friedmann solutions for
\Delta_{0}\neq 1
are time-translations of the
k=\pm 1
solutions corresponding to
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
, and hence the projection of
k\neq 0
Friedmann solutions onto solutions of (
9
) all lie on the same trajectory with
\Delta_{0}
giving the time translation.

The cases
k=-1
and
k=+1
of Theorem
42
are proven separately in subsections
13.6
and
13.7
respectively. For the proof of Theorem
41
, we require the following corollary.

Corollary 43
.

In the case
k=\pm 1
, formulas (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) and (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) both imply the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\pm}(\theta)=\boldsymbol{U}_{F}=\bigg(\frac{4}{3},\frac{2}{3},\frac{40}{27},\frac{2}{9}\bigg).

(10.33)

For the opposite limits, in the case
k=-1
, (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) imply

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\infty}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{-}(\theta)=(0,1,0,0),

(10.34)

and in the case
k=+1
, (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) imply

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{+}(\theta)=\bigg(\frac{3}{4}\pi^{2},0,0,0\bigg).

(10.35)

Proof.

The limits (
10.33
)–(
10.35
) can be confirmed numerically, but for completeness, we give a proof in the case
k=-1
based on elementary asymptotics. The argument for
k=+1
is of secondary interest to this paper and incorporated into the proof of Theorem
42
below. We first confirm the limits for
\theta\to 0
, which by (
10.23
) is equivalent to
\bar{t}\to 0
. For this it suffices to use the leading order expressions:

\displaystyle\cosh\theta

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{4}),

\displaystyle\sinh\theta

\displaystyle=\theta+\frac{1}{6}\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{5}),

as
\theta\to 0
. This then gives:

\displaystyle\cosh 2\theta-1

\displaystyle=2\theta^{2}+O(\theta^{4}),

\displaystyle\sinh 2\theta-2\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}\theta^{3}+O(\theta^{5}),

as
\theta\to 0
. Putting the leading order terms for
\theta\to 0
into (
10.19
) and using
A_{2}=-3z_{2}
gives

\displaystyle A_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2}=-2\frac{\big(\frac{4}{3}\theta^{3}\big)^{2}}{(2\theta^{2})^{3}}+H.O.T.\to-\frac{4}{9},

verifying the first limit in (
10.33
). Similarly, putting the leading order terms above into (
10.21
) for
\theta\to 0
gives

\displaystyle A_{4}=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4}=-6\frac{\big(\frac{4}{3}\theta^{3}\big)^{4}}{(2\theta^{2})^{6}}+H.O.T.\to-\frac{8}{27},

verifying the third limit in (
10.33
). Again, putting the leading order terms above into (
10.20
) for
\theta\to 0
gives

\displaystyle w_{0}=2\theta\frac{\frac{4}{3}\theta^{3}}{(2\theta^{2})^{2}}+H.O.T.\to\frac{2}{3},

verifying the second limit in (
10.33
). Finally, putting the leading order terms above into (
10.22
) for
\theta\to 0
gives

\displaystyle w_{2}=3\theta\frac{\big(\frac{4}{3}\theta^{3}\big)^{3}}{(2\theta^{2})^{5}}+H.O.T.\to\frac{2}{9},

verifying the last limit in (
10.33
).

We now confirm the limits for
\theta\to\infty
, which by (
10.23
) is equivalent to
t\to\infty
. For this it suffices to use the leading order expressions:

\displaystyle\cosh\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e^{\theta}+O(e^{-\theta}),

\displaystyle\sinh\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e^{\theta}+O(e^{-\theta}),

as
\theta\to\infty
. This then gives the leading orders of
(\cosh 2\theta-1)
and
(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)
as
\frac{1}{2}e^{2\theta}
for
\theta\to\infty
. Putting these leading order expressions into (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) easily confirms the limits on the right hand side of (
10.34
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
41
.

We begin by recalling Theorem
42
. The fact that Friedmann spacetimes are spherically symmetric solutions of the Einstein field equations which are smooth at the center for every
k
implies that they all solve the self-similar equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) in SSCNG coordinates. This justifies the expansions (
10.16
)–(
10.18
) in even powers of
\xi
, with coefficient functions of
t
, and implies that the first and second terms in their expansion, given by (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) in the case
k=-1
,
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
, and by (
10.27
)–(
10.30
) in the case
k=+1
,
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
respectively, produce unique exact solutions
(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
of the
4\times 4
system (
9
). Equation (
10.32
) shows that the
k\neq 0
Friedmann solution for
\Delta_{0}\neq 1
is a time-translation of the
k\neq 0
Friedman solution corresponding to
\Delta_{0}=\frac{4}{9}
, and hence all of the
k<0
Friedmann solutions lie on a single trajectory, with the same for
k>0
Friedmann solutions but on a different trajectory. Therefore, since
\lim_{t\to 0}\equiv\lim_{\theta\to 0}\equiv\lim_{\tau\to-\infty}
, to prove that these Friedmann solutions of (
9
) lie in the unstable manifold of
SM
, it suffices to prove that
\lim_{\theta\to 0}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\pm}(\theta)=\boldsymbol{U}_{F}
for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\pm}=(\tilde{z}_{2},\tilde{w}_{0},\tilde{z}_{4},\tilde{w}_{2})
defined in (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) and (
10.27
)–(
10.30
) respectively. Given that this follows from Corollary
43
, the proof of Theorem
41
is complete.
∎

11
The Higher Order STV-ODE

Consider now the case of the STV-ODE at orders
n\geq 3
. In this light, assume a smooth solution
(z,w)
of the STV-PDE (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) is expanded asymptotically in even powers of
\xi
as so:

\displaystyle z(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}z_{2n}(t)\xi^{2n},

\displaystyle w(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_{2n}\xi^{2n}.

We introduce the notation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{n}=(z_{2n},w_{2n-2})

for
n\geq 1
, so in terms of our earlier notation,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{1}=\boldsymbol{u}:=(z_{2},w_{0}).

In the next theorem we establish that the system of ODE, obtained by substituting the above expansions into equations (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) and collecting like powers of
\xi
, closes in
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
at each order
n\geq 1
. We then prove by induction that if

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{k}:=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k})\to M

for
k\leq n-1
in the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
closed system of ODE, then also
\boldsymbol{U}_{n}\to M
in the
n\times n
system as well, where
M
is the stable rest point in each closed
n\times n
system given by

\displaystyle M=(0,1,0,\dots,0).

We note that at
M
we have
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}=(0,1)
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}=(0,0)
for all
k\geq 2
.

Theorem 44
.

Assume a smooth solution
(z,w)
of system (
7.24
)–(
7.27
) is expanded asymptotically in even powers of
\xi
as so:

\displaystyle z(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z_{2n}(t)\xi^{2n},

(11.1)

\displaystyle w(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}w_{2n}(t)\xi^{2n},

(11.2)

\displaystyle A(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}A_{2n}(t)\xi^{2n},

(11.3)

\displaystyle D(t,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}D_{2n}(t)\xi^{2n},

(11.4)

where

\displaystyle z_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle=1.

Then substituting (
11.1
)–(
11.4
) into (
7.26
)–(
7.27
) and collecting even powers of
\xi
leads to the following system of equations:

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{2n}

\displaystyle=(2n)z_{2n}-(2n+1)\sum_{i+j+k=n}D_{2i}z_{2j}w_{2k},

(11.5)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{2n}

\displaystyle=(2n+1)w_{2n}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i+j+k=n}\hat{w}_{2i}a_{2j}D_{2k}-\sum_{i+j+k=n}(2i+1)w_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k},

(11.6)

where
\hat{w}_{2k}
and
a_{2k}
are defined by

\displaystyle\hat{w}_{2n}=\begin{cases}1,&n=0,\\
-\sum_{i+j=n-1}w_{2i}w_{2j},&n\geq 1,\end{cases}

(11.7)

and

\displaystyle\frac{A-1}{A}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{2n}\xi^{2n}.

(11.8)

respectively. Note that (
11.5
) holds for
n\geq 2
, (
11.6
) holds for
n\geq 1
and the case for
(z_{2},w_{0})
is given in system (
9
). Moreover, substituting (
11.1
) and (
11.3
) into (
7.24
) and collecting like powers of
\xi
determines
A_{2n}
in terms of
z_{2n}
according to

\displaystyle A_{2n}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2n+1}z_{2n},

(11.9)

for
n\geq 1
. Furthermore, substituting (
11.1
)–(
11.4
) into (
7.25
) and collecting like powers of
\xi
determines
D_{2n}
, as a function of
z_{2},\dots,z_{2n}
and
w_{0},\dots,w_{2n-2}
as so

\displaystyle 4nD_{2n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i+j+k+l=n-1}z_{2i}w_{2j}w_{2k}D_{2l}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
i\neq 0\end{subarray}}(z_{2i}+2A_{2i})D_{2j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
j\neq n\end{subarray}}4jA_{2i}D_{2j}

\displaystyle=-(z_{2n}+2A_{2n})+\sum_{i+j+k+l=n-1}z_{2i}w_{2j}w_{2k}D_{2l}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
i\neq 0,n\end{subarray}}(z_{2i}+2A_{2i})D_{2j}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j=n\\
j\neq n\end{subarray}}4jA_{2i}D_{2j}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2n-1}{2n+1}z_{2n}+\gamma_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n-1}),

(11.10)

for
n\geq 1
, where
\gamma_{n}
is a smooth function of
(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n-1})
. Finally, substituting expressions (
11.9
) and (
11.10
) into equations (
11.5
)–(
11.6
) results in a system of
n

2\times 2
ODE in
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
which closes for every
n\geq 1
. For each
k=1,\dots,n
, the equation for
\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
takes the form

\displaystyle t\dot{\boldsymbol{v}}_{k}=P_{k}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{k}

(11.11)

where
P_{k}
is the
2\times 2
matrix

\displaystyle P_{k}=P_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(2k+1)(1-w_{0})-1&-(2k+1)z_{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2(2k+1)}&2k(1-w_{0})-1\end{array}\right)

and:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}_{1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{0},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{q}_{k}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{q}_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k-1}).

The proof of Theorem
44
is given in Section
13.8
.

Since system (
11.11
) closes in
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
, it follows for each
n\geq 1
that equations (
11.5
)–(
11.6
) together with (
11.9
and (
11.10
) determine a closed system of
2n\times 2n
ODE in
z_{2},\dots,z_{2n}
and
w_{0},\dots,w_{2n-2}
as a function of
\tau=\ln t
. We write this as an
n\times n
system in the compact form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}P_{1}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}+\boldsymbol{q}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
P_{n}\boldsymbol{v}_{n}+\boldsymbol{q}_{n}\end{array}\right)

in unknowns
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}
, where for each
1\leq k\leq n
,
P_{k}
is a function of only
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}=(z_{2},w_{0})
and
\boldsymbol{q}_{k}
is a function of
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots\boldsymbol{v}_{k-1}
. We refer to system (
11
) as the
n^{th}
-order STV-ODE. Note that at order
n=1
we recover the
2\times 2
system (
9
) in
(z_{2},w_{0})
and at order
n=2
we recover the
4\times 4
system (
9
) in
(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
. As a consequence of the following generalization of Theorem
38
, we establish the instability of the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann spacetime, the stability of the rest point
M
and the stability of the
p=0
,
k<0
Friedmann spacetimes to perturbations of all orders. Now smooth solutions of (
11
) starting at
\tau_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
are bounded on the compact interval
[\tau_{*},\tau]
for any
\tau_{*}\leq\tau<\infty
, and since (
11
) is autonomous, solutions are preserved under the time translation
\tau\to\tau-\tau_{*}+1
. To keep things simple, and without loss of generality, we state the following theorem in terms of solutions defined for
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
, assuming initial time
\tau_{*}\geq 1
.

Theorem 45
.

Let
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau),\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\tau))
be a smooth solution of the initial value problem for the
2n\times 2n
system of ODE (
11
) starting from initial data

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}(\tau_{*})=\boldsymbol{U}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

(11.20)

Since system (
11
