) is autonomous, assume for convenience, and without loss of generality, that
\tau_{*}\geq 1
. Assume further that
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau)=(z_{2}(t),w_{0}(t))
is defined for all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau)=(0,1).

(11.21)

Then
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)
is defined for all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{v}_{k}=(0,0)

(11.22)

for
k=2,\dots,n
, that is,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to\infty}\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(0,1,0,\dots,0)=M.

(11.23)

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the system (
11
) and initial data
(\boldsymbol{U}_{*},\tau_{*})
, such that

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)-M|\leq C\tau^{n}e^{-\tau}

(11.24)

for all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
. Furthermore, there exists a
\Delta_{0}>0
and constant
C>0
such that the associated
k<0
Friedmann solution

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}^{F}(\tau)=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{F}(\tau),\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}^{F}(\tau))

satisfies

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{F}(\tau)|\leq Ce^{-\tau}

(11.25)

and

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(\tau)-\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{F}(\tau)|\leq C\tau^{k}e^{-\tau}

(11.26)

for
k=2,\dots,n
and
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
.

We have the following rather remarkable corollary, which states that any solution of the
2\times 2
system (
9
) within the domain of attraction of
M=(0,1)
can be extended arbitrarily to a global solution of (
11
) at every order
n
, with arbitrary higher order initial data. Moreover, all components higher order than
(z_{2},w_{0})
decay to zero at the rate
\frac{\ln t}{t}
. This is a restatement of Theorem
16
in the introduction.

Corollary 46
.

Assume
(z_{2}(t),w_{0}(t))
is a solution of the
2\times 2
system (
9
) with initial data

\displaystyle(z_{2}(t_{*}),w_{0}(t_{*}))

\displaystyle=(z_{2}^{*},w_{0}^{*}),

\displaystyle z_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle\geq 0,

\displaystyle t_{*}

\displaystyle>0,

(11.27)

such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}(z_{2}(t),w_{0}(t))=(0,1)=M.

Then the solution of the
2n\times 2n
system with initial data (
11.27
) augmented with the arbitrary higher order initial data

\displaystyle(z_{2k}(t_{*}),w_{2k-2}(t_{*}))

\displaystyle=(z_{2k}^{*},w_{2k-2}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\geq 2,

(11.28)

exists for all time. Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the initial data and the equations, such that all higher order components satisfy

\displaystyle\big|(z_{2k}(t),w_{2k-2}(t))-(z_{2k}^{*},w_{2k-2}^{*})\big|

\displaystyle\leq(C+1)\frac{\ln t}{t},

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\geq 2.

(11.29)

Proof.

This is Theorem
45
stated in terms of
t
instead of
\tau=\ln t
, except that the presence of
(C+1)
in (
11.29
) instead of
C
is used to cover the bounded growth of a solution during the compact time interval
t\in(t_{*},1)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
45
.

We prove this by induction. To begin, we know this holds in the
n=0
case by Theorem
38
, so we need only assume the result for cases
\leq n-1
and show that this implies case
n
. Moreover, since the
k=-1
Friedmann solutions
\boldsymbol{U}_{F}
solve system (
11
), estimates (
11.25
) and (
11.26
) for general
n\geq 1
follow from the
n=0
case once we establish (
11.23
) for
n\geq 1
. Thus it remains only to prove (
9.113
) for general
n
.

In this light, let
\boldsymbol{U}(\tau)=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau),\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\tau))
be a solution of (
11
) satisfying initial data (
11.20
), assume (
11.21
) and assume for induction that (
11.22
) and (
9.113
) hold for
k\leq n-1
. Assuming this, the proof by induction is complete once we prove (
11.22
) and (
9.113
) hold for
k=n
. For this, define
\boldsymbol{U}_{k}(\tau)=(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(\tau),\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}(\tau))
. Since system (
11
) closes in
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}
for every
k\leq n-1
, it follows that
\boldsymbol{U}_{k}
solves the
n=k
version of system (
11
) for every
k=1,\dots,n-1
. Thus by the inductive assumption, (
11.22
) and (
9.113
) hold for
k\leq n-1
, that is,
\boldsymbol{U}_{k}(\tau)\to M
and

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{U}_{k}(\tau)-M|\leq C_{k}\tau^{k}e^{-\tau}

holds for every
k\leq n-1
for some constants
C_{1},\dots,C_{n-1}
with
C_{k}=C_{k}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}^{*},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{k}^{*},\tau_{*})
, that is, depending only on the equations and the initial data
(\boldsymbol{U}_{n-1}^{*},\tau_{*})
. Thus to prove the theorem, it suffices to prove there exists a constant
C_{n}
, depending only on
C_{1},\dots,C_{n-1}
and
(\boldsymbol{U}_{n-1}^{*},\tau_{*})
, such that

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}|\leq C_{n}\tau^{n}e^{-\tau}.

So for induction, assume without loss of generality, that

\displaystyle z_{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=a(\tau)\tau e^{-\tau},

(11.30)

\displaystyle w_{0}(\tau)

\displaystyle=1+b(\tau)\tau e^{-\tau},

(11.31)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{U}_{n-1}(\tau)

\displaystyle=M+\boldsymbol{V}_{n-1}(\tau)\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau},

(11.32)

where

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n-1},

\displaystyle|b(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n-1},

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{V}_{n-1}(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n-1},

(11.33)

for all
\tau\geq\tau_{*}
. Putting (
11.30
) into system (
11
) and assuming (
11.33
), we find that the
n^{th}
equation for
\boldsymbol{v}_{n}=(z_{2n},w_{2n-2})=(u,v)
in system (
11
) takes the form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
-\frac{1}{10}&-1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\right)+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}A(\tau)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u\\
v\end{array}\right)+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}B(\tau),

with

\displaystyle u(1)

\displaystyle=u_{*},

\displaystyle v(1)

\displaystyle=v_{*},

and where
u=z_{2n}
and
v=w_{2n-2}
are treated as unknowns. Moreover,
A
and
B
are
2\times 2
matrices determined by
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{v}_{n-1}
and assumed without loss of generality to satisfy the induction hypothesis in the form

\displaystyle\|A\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n-1},

\displaystyle\|B\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{n-1}.

(11.42)

We obtain estimate (
9.113
) from the theory of scalar first-order linear equations. For this we need a preliminary supnorm estimate on
|u|+|v|
to derive an estimate for
|u|
, independent of
v
, from the first equation in (
11
) and an estimate for
|v|
, independent of
u
, from the second equation in (
11
). For this, (
11
) implies the two estimates:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}|u|

\displaystyle\leq-|u|+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}\|A\||v|+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}\|B\|,

(11.43)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}|v|

\displaystyle\leq-|v|+\frac{1}{10}|u|+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}\|B\|.

(11.44)

Adding positive terms to the right hand sides of (
11.43
) and (
11.44
) and then adding the equations gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(|u|+|v|)\leq-\frac{9}{10}(|u|+|v|)+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}\|A\|(|u|+|v|)+\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}\|B\|,

which in light of (
11.42
) yields the linear Grönwall estimate

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}w\leq g(\tau)w+f(\tau),

(11.45)

where:

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=|u|+|v|,

\displaystyle g(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{10}+C_{n-1}\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau},

\displaystyle f(\tau)

\displaystyle=C_{n-1}\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau}.

Multiplying (
11.45
) through by the integrating factor
e^{-\int_{1}^{\tau}g(s)ds}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}\Big(e^{-\int_{1}^{\tau}g(s)ds}w\Big)\leq e^{-\int_{1}^{\tau}g(s)ds}f(\tau),

which integrates to

\displaystyle w(\tau)\leq e^{-\int_{1}^{\tau}g(z)dz}\Bigg(e^{\tau_{*}}w_{*}+\int_{1}^{\tau}f(z)\bigg(-\int_{1}^{z}g(s)ds\bigg)dz\Bigg).

(11.46)

Now recall that

\displaystyle I_{k}=\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{k}e^{-s}ds

integrates by parts to

\displaystyle I_{k}=kI_{k-1}=k(k-1)I_{k-2}=\dots=k!,

thus

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\tau}g(s)ds\leq\int_{1}^{\tau}-\frac{9}{10}+C_{n-1}s^{n-1}e^{-s}\ ds\leq-\frac{9}{10}(\tau-1)+C_{n-1}(n-1)!.

Using this in (
11.46
) gives the estimate

\displaystyle w(\tau)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C_{n-1}(n-1)!}e^{-\frac{9}{10}(\tau-1)}\big(e^{\tau_{*}}w_{*}+C_{n-1}^{2}(n-1)!\big)

\displaystyle\leq e^{C_{n-1}(n-1)!}\big(e^{\tau_{*}}w_{*}+C_{n-1}^{2}(n-1)!\big)=:\bar{C},

(11.47)

where
\bar{C}
depends only on
C_{n-1}
and the initial data.

Using the estimate (
11.47
) for
|v|
in (
11.43
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}|u|\leq-|u|+C_{n-1}(\bar{C}+1)\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau},

which by the integrating factor method yields

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}(e^{\tau}|u|)\leq C_{n-1}(\bar{C}+1)\tau^{n-1},

and integrates to

\displaystyle|u(\tau)|\leq e^{-\tau}\bigg(e^{\tau_{*}}|u_{*}|+\frac{C_{n-1}(\bar{C}+1)}{n}\tau^{n}\bigg)\leq\bar{C}_{u}\tau^{n}e^{-\tau},

where
\tau\geq\tau_{*}\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{u}:=e^{\tau_{*}}|u_{*}|+\frac{C_{n-1}(\bar{C}+1)}{n}.

Alternatively, using (
11.47
) to estimate
u
in the
v
-equation (
11.44
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}|v|\leq-|v|+\bigg(\frac{1}{10}\bar{C}_{u}+C_{n+1}\bigg)\tau^{n-1}e^{-\tau},

which integrates as above to

\displaystyle|v(\tau)|\leq e^{-\tau}\bigg(e^{\tau_{*}}|v_{*}|+\frac{\frac{1}{10}\bar{C}_{u}+C_{n+1}}{n}\tau^{n}\bigg)\leq\bar{C}_{v}\tau^{n}e^{-\tau},

where again
\tau\geq\tau_{*}\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{v}:=e^{\tau_{*}}|v_{*}|+\frac{\frac{1}{10}\bar{C}_{u}+C_{n+1}}{n}.

Now by setting

\displaystyle C_{n}=\sqrt{\bar{C}_{u}^{2}+\bar{C}_{v}^{2}},

we conclude

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{v}_{n}(\tau)|=\sqrt{|u(\tau)|^{2}+|v(\tau)|^{2}}\leq\sqrt{\bar{C}_{u}^{2}+\bar{C}_{v}^{2}}\ \tau^{n}e^{-\tau}=C_{n+1}\tau^{n}e^{-\tau},

from which estimate (
9.113
) follows. This completes the induction step and thereby completes the proof.
∎

In the next corollary of Theorem
45
, we explicitly compute system (
11.5
)–(
11.6
) for
n=1,2,3
.

Corollary 47
.

Extracting the leading order terms from the right hand side of (
11.5
), we obtain the equivalent form (also see (
13.125
) below)

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{2n}=\big((2n+1)(1-w_{0})-1\big)z_{2n}-(2n+1)z_{2}w_{2n-2}\delta_{1}(n)-(2n+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=n\\
i\neq n,j\neq n-1\end{subarray}}z_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k},

(11.48)

where

\displaystyle\delta_{1}(n)=\begin{cases}1,&n=1,\\
0,&n\neq 1.\end{cases}

From (
11.48
) we compute the equations for
n=1,2,3
:

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0},

(11.49)

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{4}

\displaystyle=4z_{4}-5(z_{4}w_{0}+z_{2}w_{2}+z_{2}w_{0}D_{2}),

(11.50)

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{6}

\displaystyle=6z_{6}-7(z_{6}w_{0}+z_{2}w_{4}+z_{2}w_{0}D_{4}+z_{2}w_{2}D_{2}+z_{4}w_{0}D_{2}+z_{4}w_{2}).

(11.51)

Similarly, extracting the leading order terms from the right hand side of (
11.6
), we obtain the equivalent form

t\dot{w}_{2n}=-\frac{1}{2(2n+3)}z_{2n+2}+\big((2n+2)(1-w_{0})-1\big)w_{2n}+\frac{1}{2}(a_{2n}-A_{2n+2})\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=n\\
j\neq n\end{subarray}}\hat{w}_{2i}a_{2j}D_{2k}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=n\\
i\neq n,j\neq n\end{subarray}}(2i+1)w_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k}.

(11.52)

Again, from (
11.52
) we compute the equations for
n=1,2,3
:

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}+w-w_{0}^{2},

(11.53)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+3w_{2}-4w_{0}w_{2}-\frac{1}{2}w_{0}^{2}A_{2}-\frac{1}{2}A_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}D_{2}-w_{0}^{2}D_{2},

(11.54)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{14}z_{6}+5w_{4}-6w_{0}w_{4}-\frac{1}{2}w_{0}^{2}(A_{4}-A_{2}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}w_{0}^{2}A_{2}D_{2}

\displaystyle-w_{0}w_{2}A_{2}+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}D_{4}+\frac{1}{2}(A_{4}-A_{2}^{2})D_{2}-A_{2}A_{4}+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle-w_{0}^{2}D_{4}-4w_{0}w_{2}D_{2}-3w_{2}^{2}.

(11.55)

Moreover,

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2},

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4},

\displaystyle A_{6}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{7}z_{6},

(11.56)

and:

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}z_{2},

(11.57)

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{40}z_{4}+\frac{1}{8}z_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{96}z_{2}^{2},

(11.58)

\displaystyle D_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{12}\bigg(z_{4}w_{0}^{2}+2z_{2}w_{0}w_{2}+\frac{5}{24}z_{2}^{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{23}{120}z_{2}z_{4}-\frac{7}{288}z_{2}^{3}-\frac{5}{7}z_{6}\bigg).

(11.59)

Proof.

Equations (
11.48
) and (
11.52
) are derived from (
13.125
) and (
13.127
) respectively. To eliminate
\hat{w}_{2i}a_{2j}D_{2k}
from (
11.52
), close the equations in
z_{2k}
,
w_{2k}
,
A_{2k}
and
D_{2k}
at orders
n=1,2,3
and use the expression

\displaystyle\sum_{i+j+k=n}\hat{w}_{2i}a_{2j}D_{2k}

\displaystyle=A_{2}-w_{0}^{2}A_{2}-A_{2}^{2}+A_{2}D_{2}+A_{4}-w_{0}^{2}(A_{4}-A_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle-w_{0}^{2}A_{2}D_{2}-2w_{0}w_{2}A_{2}+A_{2}D_{4}+A_{4}-A_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-2A_{2}A_{4}+A_{2}^{3}+A_{6}.

By this we arrive at (
11.49
)–(
11.51
) and (
11.53
)–(
11.55
).

Equations (
11.56
) and (
11.58
) follow from (
11.9
) and (
11.10
). As an example, we consider the case of
D_{6}
. From (
11.10
) we have

\displaystyle 12D_{6}

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i,j,k,l)\in X}z_{2i}w_{2j}w_{2k}D_{2l}-\sum_{(i,j)\in Y}\Big((z_{2i}+2A_{2i})D_{2j}-4jA_{2i}D_{2j}\Big)

\displaystyle=z_{4}w_{0}^{2}+2z_{2}w_{0}w_{2}+z_{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{2}-z_{2}D_{4}-10A_{2}D_{4}-z_{4}D_{2}+6A_{4}D_{2}-z_{6}+2A_{6}

\displaystyle=z_{4}w_{0}^{2}+2z_{2}w_{0}w_{2}+z_{2}w_{0}^{2}D_{2}+\frac{7}{3}z_{2}D_{4}+\frac{1}{5}z_{4}D_{2}-\frac{5}{7}z_{6}

\displaystyle=z_{4}w_{0}^{2}+2z_{2}w_{0}w_{2}+\frac{5}{24}z_{2}^{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{23}{120}z_{2}z_{4}-\frac{7}{288}z_{2}^{3}-\frac{5}{7}z_{6},

where

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\{(2,0,0,0),(1,0,0,0),(1,0,1,0),(1,1,0,0)\},

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\{(1,2),(2,1),(3,0)\},

which confirms (
11.59
).
∎

Equations (
11.56
)–(
11.59
), recorded in (
2.32
)–(
2.35
) of the introduction, express the
A_{i}
’s and
D_{j}
’s in terms of
w_{i}
’s and
z_{j}
’s. Using these to eliminate the
A_{i}
’s and
D_{j}
’s from equations (
11.49
)–(
11.51
) and (
11.53
)–(
11.55
) leads, after simplification, to equations (
2.26
)–(
2.31
) of the introduction. This, together with (
11.56
)–(
11.59
), then establishes Theorem
12
, our final result given in the introduction.

12
Pure Eigenvalue Solutions

Recall from Section
1.5
and (
1.19
) that the eigenvalues of our expansion in
\xi
about
SM
take the form:

\displaystyle\lambda_{An}

\displaystyle=\frac{2n}{3},

\displaystyle\lambda_{Bn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(2n-5).

Each eigenvalue introduces a free parameter into the expansion, so there are two additional free parameters at each order. At orders
n=1
and
n=2
we know the negative eigenvalues do not appear in the expansion of a
k<0
Friedmann solution, since this would mean the trajectory does not originate at the fixed point
SM
. Furthermore, we know from Section
9
that the
k<0
Friedmann solutions at leading order (
n=1
) have a one-to-one correspondence with trajectories emanating from the unstable manifold of
SM
on the underdense side. Thus the free parameter associated with the first positive eigenvalue,
\lambda_{A1}
, is related to the single
k<0
Friedmann free parameter
\Delta_{0}
. This free parameter will appear at higher orders in the expansion as well, the question is whether the higher order coefficients of the expansion of the
k<0
Friedmann solution are generated purely by
\Delta_{0}
, or whether the parameters that appear at higher orders introduce additional contributions of
\Delta_{0}
. Put another way, we know that the
k<0
Friedmann solution has a single parameter freedom, but we do not know if the additional parameters that enter generically in the expansion are absent, making the
k<0
Friedmann solution a pure eigenvector solution, or whether these additional parameters are functions of
\Delta_{0}
, implying the
k<0
Friedmann solution is not generated by the single leading order parameter. We conjecture that it is the former that is true, with the following theorem confirming this up to order
n=3
.

Theorem 48
.

Let
(\bar{t},\xi)
represent SSCNG coordinates, with
\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}
. Then smooth perturbations of
SM
take the form:

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=1+A_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+A_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+A_{6}(\bar{t})\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8}),

(12.1)

\displaystyle D(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=1+D_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+D_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+D_{6}(\bar{t})\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8}),

(12.2)

\displaystyle z(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=z_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+z_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+z_{6}(\bar{t})\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8}),

(12.3)

\displaystyle w(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=w_{0}(\bar{t})+w_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+w_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(12.4)

where:

\displaystyle A_{2}(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2}(\bar{t}),

\displaystyle A_{4}(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4}(\bar{t}),

\displaystyle A_{6}(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{7}z_{6}(\bar{t}),

\displaystyle D_{2}(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}(\bar{t}),

\displaystyle D_{4}(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}\left(w_{0}^{2}(\bar{t})-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}(\bar{t})\right)z_{2}(\bar{t})-\frac{3}{40}z_{4}(\bar{t}),

\displaystyle D_{6}(\bar{t})=\frac{1}{6}\left(w_{0}(\bar{t})w_{2}(\bar{t})+\frac{5}{48}w_{0}^{2}(\bar{t})z_{2}(\bar{t})-\frac{7}{576}z_{2}^{2}(\bar{t})\right)z_{2}(\bar{t})+\frac{1}{12}\left(w_{0}^{2}(\bar{t})-\frac{23}{120}z_{2}(\bar{t})\right)z_{4}(\bar{t})-\frac{5}{84}z_{6}(\bar{t}),

and:

\displaystyle z_{2}(\bar{t})=\frac{4}{3}-6a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\frac{153}{7}a^{2}\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}-\frac{1023}{14}a^{3}\bar{t}^{2}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.5)

\displaystyle z_{4}(\bar{t})=\frac{40}{27}-\frac{20}{9}a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\left(\frac{100}{21}a^{2}+10b\right)\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\left(\frac{1000}{21}a^{3}+\frac{720}{7}ab\right)\bar{t}^{2}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.6)

z_{6}(\bar{t})=\frac{448}{243}-\frac{196}{81}a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\left(\frac{58}{27}a^{2}+\frac{98}{9}b\right)\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\left(\frac{77}{3}a^{3}+\frac{211}{2}ab-14c\right)\bar{t}^{2}\\
+\frac{28}{3}\alpha\bar{t}^{\frac{1}{3}}-84\alpha a\bar{t}+465\alpha a^{2}\bar{t}^{\frac{5}{3}}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.7)

\displaystyle w_{0}(\bar{t})=\frac{2}{3}+a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{39}{14}a^{2}\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\frac{213}{28}a^{3}\bar{t}^{2}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.8)

\displaystyle w_{2}(\bar{t})=\frac{2}{9}-\frac{2}{3}a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\left(\frac{17}{14}a^{2}+b\right)\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}-\frac{60}{7}ab\bar{t}^{2}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.9)

\displaystyle w_{4}(\bar{t})=\frac{13}{81}-\frac{35}{54}a\bar{t}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\left(\frac{295}{126}a^{2}-\frac{7}{18}b\right)\bar{t}^{\frac{4}{3}}-\left(\frac{70}{9}a^{3}-c\right)\bar{t}^{2}+\alpha\bar{t}^{\frac{1}{3}}-\frac{11}{2}\alpha a\bar{t}+\frac{99}{4}\alpha a^{2}\bar{t}^{\frac{5}{3}}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

(12.10)

where
a
,
b
,
c
and
\alpha
are constants. In particular, the
k=-1
Friedmann spacetime satisfies
b=c=0
and
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=0
, with

\displaystyle a^{3}=\frac{2}{375}\Delta_{0}^{-2},

that is, the
k=-1
Friedmann spacetime is a pure eigenvalue solution up to order
\xi^{6}
.

Proof.

Substituting series (
12.1
)–(
12.4
) into the STV PDE (
7.24
)–(
7.27
), we immediately obtain the algebraic relations for
A_{2}
,
A_{4}
,
A_{6}
,
D_{2}
,
D_{4}
and
D_{6}
. The remaining equations are then given by:

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{w}_{0}

\displaystyle=(1-w_{0})w_{0}-\frac{1}{6}z_{2},

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=(2-3w_{0})z_{2},

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=(3-4w_{0})w_{2}-\frac{1}{10}z_{4}+\frac{1}{4}\left(w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{6}z_{2}\right)z_{2},

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{z}_{4}

\displaystyle=(4-5w_{0})z_{4}-5z_{2}w_{2}+\frac{5}{12}z_{2}^{2}w_{0},

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{w}_{4}

\displaystyle=(5-6w_{0})w_{4}-\frac{1}{14}z_{6}+\frac{7}{40}\left(w_{0}^{2}-\frac{11}{42}z_{2}\right)z_{4}-3\left(w_{2}-\frac{2}{9}z_{2}w_{0}\right)w_{2}-\frac{1}{8}\left(w_{0}^{4}-\frac{1}{4}w_{0}^{2}z_{2}+\frac{7}{72}z_{2}^{2}\right)z_{2},

\displaystyle\bar{t}\dot{z}_{6}

\displaystyle=(6-7w_{0})z_{6}-7z_{2}w_{4}+\frac{7}{12}z_{2}^{2}w_{2}-7\left(w_{2}-\frac{19}{120}z_{2}w_{0}\right)z_{4}-\frac{7}{8}\left(w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}\right)w_{0}z_{2}^{2}.

We know from Theorem
41
that the leading order terms of
w_{0}
and
z_{2}
in the limit
\bar{t}\to 0
are proportional to

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{A1}(\tau)}=\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A1}},

where
\lambda_{A1}=\frac{2}{3}
is the positive eigenvalue of the
n=1
system and
\tau=\ln\bar{t}
. We have already seen that the negative eigenvalue
\lambda_{B1}
is eliminated by setting
time since the Big Bang
and thus does not feature in the leading order analysis. For
n=2
, if we denote
U(\bar{t})=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2})
, then the leading order behavior as
\bar{t}\to 0
becomes

\displaystyle U(\bar{t})=a\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A1}}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+b\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A2}}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2},

where
\lambda_{A2}=\frac{4}{3}
. We note that
\lambda_{B2}
is the only negative eigenvalue for
n>1
since in general:

\displaystyle\lambda_{An}

\displaystyle=\frac{2n}{3},

\displaystyle\lambda_{Bn}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(2n-5).

If we denote
U(\bar{t})=(z_{2},w_{0},z_{4},w_{2},z_{6},w_{4})
, then the leading order behavior as
\bar{t}\to 0
is given in general by

\displaystyle U(\bar{t})

\displaystyle=a\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A1}}\boldsymbol{R}_{A1}+b\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A2}}\boldsymbol{R}_{A2}+c\bar{t}^{\lambda_{A3}}\boldsymbol{R}_{A3}

\displaystyle+\alpha\bar{t}^{\lambda_{B3}}\boldsymbol{R}_{B3}+\beta\bar{t}^{\lambda_{B4}}\boldsymbol{R}_{B4}+\gamma\bar{t}^{\lambda_{B5}}\boldsymbol{R}_{B5}+O(\bar{t}^{\frac{7}{3}}),

where
a
,
b
,
c
,
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
are free parameters. With this knowledge, we can compute the Taylor series of
w_{0}
,
w_{2}
,
w_{4}
,
z_{2}
,
z_{4}
and
z_{6}
to yield (
12.5
)–(
12.10
). What remains is to show that the
k=-1
Friedmann spacetime parameters satisfy
b=c=0
and
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=0
, where

\displaystyle a^{3}=\frac{2}{375}\Delta_{0}^{-2}.

Recall from Theorem
24
that:

\displaystyle 1-A

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{3}\rho\bar{r}^{2}=\frac{8\bar{\chi}^{2}\xi^{2}}{(\cosh 2\Theta-1)^{3}},

(12.11)

\displaystyle\sqrt{AB}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{1+r^{2}}}{\frac{\partial\bar{t}}{\partial t}(t,r)}=\frac{\sqrt{1+\frac{\bar{\chi}^{2}\xi^{2}}{\sinh^{4}\Theta}}}{\frac{\partial\bar{\chi}}{\partial\chi}(\chi,r)},

(12.12)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{R}r}{\sqrt{1+r^{2}}}=\frac{\frac{\bar{\chi}\xi}{\sinh^{2}\Theta}\coth\Theta}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\bar{\chi}^{2}\xi^{2}}{\sinh^{4}\Theta}}},

(12.13)

where:

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}(\sinh 2\Theta-2\Theta),

(12.14)

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{t}}{\Delta_{0}},

(12.15)

and:

\displaystyle\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\sqrt[4]{1+\frac{\bar{\chi}^{2}\xi^{2}}{\sinh^{4}\Theta}}\cosh\Theta,

(12.16)

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\chi}\xi}{\sinh^{2}\Theta}.

(12.17)

Note that
\Theta=\Theta(\chi)
is the inverse of (
12.14
), whereas
\Theta(\bar{\chi})
is the inverse of the same expression but with
\chi
replaced with
\bar{\chi}
. Now with a nontrivial amount of algebra we can write (
12.11
)–(
12.13
) as:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\xi^{2}\bar{\chi}^{2}}{\sinh^{6}\Theta},

(12.18)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=\frac{\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})\sinh\Theta}{\sinh\Theta(\bar{\chi})\cosh\Theta},

(12.19)

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{\chi}\cosh^{3}\Theta}{\cosh^{2}\Theta(\bar{\chi})\sinh^{3}\Theta},

(12.20)

along with

\displaystyle z=\frac{3(1-A)}{1-\xi^{2}w^{2}}.

(12.21)

The strategy is to write
A
,
D
,
w
and
z
as a series of the form (
12.1
)–(
12.4
). From this, the series of
w_{2n-2}(\bar{t})
and
z_{2n}(\bar{t})
can be deduced and compared to the general series (
12.5
)–(
12.10
). The first step is thus to write variables
A
,
D
,
w
and
z
as a series in
(\bar{\chi},\xi)
, which requires expanding
\Theta=\Theta(\chi)
as a series in
(\bar{\chi},\xi)
. We can compute this using relation (
12.16
) to yield

\displaystyle\Theta(\chi)

\displaystyle=\Theta(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\chi}^{2}\frac{\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{5}\Theta(\bar{\chi})}\xi^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{32}\bar{\chi}^{4}\frac{8\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})+\cosh 3\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{11}\Theta(\bar{\chi})}\xi^{4}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{384}\bar{\chi}^{6}\frac{116+75\cosh 2\Theta(\bar{\chi})+4\cosh 4\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{17}\Theta(\bar{\chi})}\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8}).

With this series, we can expand (
12.18
)–(
12.21
) to take the form given by (
12.1
)–(
12.4
), recalling that
\bar{t}=\Delta_{0}\bar{\chi}
. Computing these series we obtain:

\displaystyle w_{0}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}\frac{\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{3}\Theta(\bar{\chi})},

(12.22)

\displaystyle z_{2}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=3\bar{\chi}^{2}\frac{1}{\sinh^{6}\Theta(\bar{\chi})},

(12.23)

\displaystyle w_{2}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}\bar{\chi}^{3}\frac{\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{9}\Theta(\bar{\chi})},

(12.24)

\displaystyle z_{4}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{15}{2}\bar{\chi}^{4}\frac{\cosh^{2}\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{12}\Theta(\bar{\chi})},

(12.25)

\displaystyle w_{4}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{64}\bar{\chi}^{5}\frac{23\cosh\Theta(\bar{\chi})+3\cosh 3\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{15}\Theta(\bar{\chi})},

(12.26)

\displaystyle z_{6}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{21}{16}\bar{\chi}^{6}\frac{11+5\cosh 2\Theta(\bar{\chi})}{\sinh^{18}\Theta(\bar{\chi})}\cosh^{2}\Theta(\bar{\chi}).

(12.27)

Now noting that

\displaystyle\Theta(\bar{\chi})=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{1}{3}}-\frac{1}{10}\bar{\chi}+\frac{3}{175}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{5}{3}}-\frac{1}{175}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{7}{3}}+O(\bar{\chi}^{3}),

we can thus expand (
12.22
)–(
12.27
) as a series in
\bar{\chi}
to obtain:

\displaystyle w_{0}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3}+\frac{1}{5}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{13}{175}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\frac{71}{1750}\bar{\chi}^{2}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

\displaystyle z_{2}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}-\frac{2}{5}(18)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\frac{51}{175}(12)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}-\frac{341}{875}\bar{\chi}^{2}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

\displaystyle w_{2}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{9}-\frac{2}{15}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\frac{17}{175}(18)^{-\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

\displaystyle z_{4}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{40}{27}-\frac{4}{9}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{4}{21}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\frac{16}{63}\bar{\chi}^{2}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

\displaystyle w_{4}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{13}{81}-\frac{7}{27}(12)^{-\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}+\frac{59}{315}(18)^{-\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}-\frac{28}{675}\bar{\chi}^{2}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}),

\displaystyle z_{6}(\bar{\chi})

\displaystyle=\frac{448}{243}-\frac{196}{405}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{58}{675}\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\bar{\chi}^{\frac{4}{3}}+\frac{154}{1125}\bar{\chi}^{2}+O(\bar{\chi}^{\frac{8}{3}}).

Finally, we see that by identifying

\displaystyle a^{3}=\frac{2}{375}\Delta_{0}^{-2},

we obtain (
12.5
)–(
12.10
) with
b=c=0
and
\alpha=\beta=\gamma=0
.
∎

13
Appendix: Proofs of the Main Theorems

13.1
Proof of Theorem
22
: Transformation to SSCNG

First note that by direct differentiation:

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}g=\frac{\lambda}{\dot{R}R}\Phi,

(13.1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{r}

\displaystyle=hg^{\prime}=\frac{\lambda r}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi.

(13.2)

Letting
\boldsymbol{x}=(t,r)
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}=(\hat{t},\bar{r})
, the inverse Jacobian is given by

\displaystyle J^{-1}=\frac{\partial\hat{x}^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\mu}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi_{t}&\Phi_{r}\\
\dot{R}r&R\end{array}\right)^{\nu}_{\mu},

so

\displaystyle J=\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial\hat{x}^{\nu}}=\frac{1}{|J^{-1}|}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}R&-\Phi_{r}\\
-\dot{R}r&\Phi_{t}\end{array}\right)^{\mu}_{\nu},

where

\displaystyle|J^{-1}|=|R\Phi_{t}-\dot{R}r\Phi_{r}|.

Thus

\displaystyle\hat{g}=J^{T}gJ

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J^{-1}|^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}R&-\dot{R}r\\
-\Phi_{r}&\Phi_{t}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&0\\
0&\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}R&-\Phi_{r}\\
-\dot{R}r&\Phi_{t}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J^{-1}|^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-R&-\frac{\dot{R}R^{2}r}{1-kr^{2}}\\
\Phi_{r}&\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}R&-\Phi_{r}\\
-\dot{R}r&\Phi_{t}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|J^{-1}|^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{(\dot{R}Rr)^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}-R^{2}&R\Phi_{r}-\frac{\dot{R}R^{2}r}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}\\
R\Phi_{r}-\frac{\dot{R}R^{2}r}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}&\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}^{2}-\Phi_{r}^{2}\end{array}\right).

We first verify that the middle term
\hat{g}_{01}
in (
13.1
) vanishes when
\Phi(t,r)=h(t)g(r)
, with
g
and
h
given in (
5.33
). To see this we derive (
5.33
) from the condition

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{01}=R\Phi_{r}-\frac{\dot{R}R^{2}r}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}=0,

which gives

\displaystyle\frac{1-kr^{2}}{r}\frac{g^{\prime}}{g}=\lambda=\dot{R}R\frac{h^{\prime}}{h}

for some positive constant
\lambda
. Integrating then gives:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\dot{R}(s)R(s)}},

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\int_{0}^{r}\frac{sds}{1-ks^{2}}},

in agreement with (
5.33
).

To establish (
5.35
) and (
5.36
), let
\hat{g}_{00}=-\hat{B}
and
\hat{g}_{11}=\hat{A}^{-1}
, then straightforward substitutions give:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\hat{A}(\hat{t},\bar{r})}

\displaystyle=\frac{R^{2}\Phi_{t}^{2}-(1-kr^{2})\Phi_{r}^{2}}{(R\Phi_{t}-\dot{R}r\Phi_{r})^{2}(1-kr^{2})}=1-kr^{2}-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2},

(13.19)

\displaystyle\hat{B}(\hat{t},\bar{r})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{|J^{-1}|^{2}}\bigg(\frac{(\dot{R}Rr)^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}-R^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-kr^{2})R^{2}-(\dot{R}Rr)^{2}}{(R\Phi_{t}-\dot{R}r\Phi_{r})^{2}(1-kr^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-kr^{2}}{1-kr^{2}-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2}}\bigg(\frac{R^{2}\dot{R}^{2}}{\lambda^{2}\Phi^{2}}\bigg).

(13.20)

This implies

\displaystyle B=\frac{1}{F^{\prime}(\Phi)^{2}}\hat{B}=\frac{1}{(F^{\prime}(\Phi)\Phi_{t})^{2}}\frac{1-kr^{2}}{1-kr^{2}-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2}},

verifying (
5.36
). To obtain the expression for
A(\bar{t},\bar{r})
for the Friedmann metrics in SSCNG, note that, assuming (
5.31
), equation (
13.1
) gives the formula

\displaystyle\frac{1}{A}=\frac{(F^{\prime})^{2}}{|J^{-1}|^{2}}\bigg(\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}^{2}-\Phi_{r}^{2}\bigg),

with

\displaystyle|J^{-1}|=|F^{\prime}||R\Phi_{t}-\dot{R}r\Phi_{r}|,

so

\displaystyle A=\frac{(R\Phi_{t}-\dot{R}r\Phi_{r})^{2}}{\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\Phi_{t}^{2}-\Phi_{r}^{2}}.

(13.21)

Putting (
13.1
) and (
13.2
) into (
13.21
) gives

\displaystyle A=\frac{\Big(\frac{\lambda R\Phi}{\dot{R}R}-\frac{\dot{R}r\lambda r\Phi}{1-kr^{2}}\Big)^{2}}{\frac{R^{2}}{1-kr^{2}}\Big(\frac{\lambda\Phi}{\dot{R}R}\Big)^{2}-\Big(\frac{\lambda r\Phi}{1-kr^{2}}\Big)^{2}}=\frac{(1-kr^{2}-\dot{R}^{2}r^{2})^{2}}{1-kr^{2}-\dot{R}^{2}r^{2}},

which takes the final form

\displaystyle A=1-kr^{2}-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2},

(13.22)

agreeing with (
5.35
). Thus (
5.35
) and (
5.36
) are confirmed.

It remains to establish (
5.38
). Since the four-velocity equals
\boldsymbol{e}_{0}=(1,0)
and the fluid velocity vanishes in comoving coordinates
(t,r)
, the formula for
J^{-1}
in (
13.1
) gives

\displaystyle\hat{u}^{\nu}=(J^{-1})^{\nu}_{\mu}u^{\mu}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Phi_{t}\\
\dot{R}r\end{array}\right),

and (
13.19
)–(
13.20
) give

\displaystyle\sqrt{AB}=\frac{\sqrt{1-kr^{2}}}{\frac{\partial\bar{t}}{\partial t}(t,r)},

which verifies (
5.37
). Putting this together with (
13.1
) into the formula for
\hat{v}
gives

\displaystyle\hat{v}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{A}\hat{B}}}\frac{\hat{u}^{1}}{\hat{u}^{0}}=\frac{\dot{R}r}{\sqrt{1-kr^{2}}},

(13.23)

where

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\bar{B}=\frac{1}{(F^{\prime}(\hat{t}))^{2}}\hat{B},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\bar{A}=\hat{A}.

To verify (
5.38
), note that the mapping from
(\bar{t},\bar{r})=(F(\hat{t}),\hat{r})
involves only a change in time. Thus, using the notation
\bar{\boldsymbol{u}}=\boldsymbol{u}
, we have
u^{0}=F^{\prime}(\hat{t})\hat{t}
,
u^{0}=\hat{u}^{0}
and (
13.23
), so plugging this into the formula
v=\frac{1}{\sqrt{AB}}\frac{u^{1}}{u^{0}}
, the two factors of
F^{\prime}(\hat{t})
cancel and we see that
v=\hat{v}
.

Finally, note that
B(\bar{t},0)=1
determines
F
in (
5.31
) to be (
5.34
), as it must, because in the SSC gauge
B(\bar{t},0)=1
, SSC time
\bar{t}
and comoving time
t
both measure proper time at
r=0
, so
\bar{t}=F(h(t))=t
and
F(y)=h^{-1}(y)
. This completes the proof of Theorem
22
.∎

13.2
Proof of Theorem
30
: The Friedmann Spacetime in SSCNG Coordinates

Using the
k=0
version of (
5.31
)–(
5.34
) of Theorem
22
, let
R(t)
denote the cosmological scale factor and define the coordinate transformation:

\displaystyle\bar{t}

\displaystyle=F(h(t)g(r)),

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=R(t)r,

(13.24)

where:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d\tau}{\dot{R}(\tau)R(\tau)}},

(13.25)

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\lambda}{2}r^{2}},

(13.26)

\displaystyle F(y)

\displaystyle=h^{-1}(y),

(13.27)

and we use the notation

\displaystyle y=\hat{t}=\Phi(t,r)=h(t)g(r).

By Theorem
22
, (
13.24
) transforms metric (
5.1
) over to SSC form (
5.29
), the normalized gauge condition
B(\bar{t},0)=1
holds and:

\displaystyle A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=1-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2},

(13.28)

\displaystyle B_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(F(\Phi)_{t})^{2}(1-H^{2}\bar{r}^{2})}.

(13.29)

We now use formulas (
6.6
)–(
6.8
) for
R(t)
,
H(t)
and
\rho(t)
of Theorem
29
, together with the comoving velocity condition, applicable to the
p=0
,
k=0
Friedmann metric in comoving coordinates
(t,r)
. Starting with (
6.6
),

\displaystyle R(t)=\bigg(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\bigg)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},

where

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{4}{3(1+\sigma)}.

Differentiating yields

\displaystyle\dot{R}(t)=\frac{\alpha}{2t_{0}}\bigg(\frac{t}{t_{0}}\bigg)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}

and substituting this into (
13.25
) and integrating gives

\displaystyle h(t)=e^{\lambda\big(\frac{2t_{0}^{\alpha}}{\alpha(2-\alpha)}t^{2-\alpha}\big)}.

From this we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{t}=\Phi(t,r)=h(t)g(r)=e^{\lambda\big(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\big)\frac{2t_{0}^{2}}{\alpha(2-\alpha)}t^{2-\alpha}}

and

\displaystyle F(y)=h^{-1}(y)=\bigg(\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)\ln(y)}{2t_{0}^{\alpha}\lambda}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2-\alpha}}.

Thus by simple algebra,

\displaystyle\bar{t}=h^{-1}(h(t)g(r))=h^{-1}(\hat{t})=1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}.

This confirms that the transformation (
6.10
) is equivalent to (
13.24
).

Consider now the formula (
13.28
). Equation (
6.7
) gives the Friedmann formula for the Hubble constant

\displaystyle H=\frac{2}{3(1+\sigma)}\frac{1}{t}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\frac{1}{t}.

Substituting this into (
13.28
) and simplifying confirms (
6.13
). To confirm (
13.29
), we calculate

\displaystyle F(\Phi(t,r))_{t}=\frac{dh^{-1}}{d\hat{t}}\frac{d\hat{t}}{dt}=\bigg(\frac{\ln\hat{t}}{\lambda}\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{2t_{0}^{\alpha}}\bigg)^{-\frac{1-\alpha}{2-\alpha}}t^{1-\alpha},

which simplifies to

\displaystyle F(\Phi(t,r))_{t}=\bigg(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\bigg)^{-\frac{1-\alpha}{2-\alpha}}.

Using the above formulas in (
13.29
) then gives

\displaystyle B_{\sigma}=\frac{1}{(1-H\bar{r}^{2})F(h(t)g(r))_{t}^{2}}=\frac{\Big(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\Big)^{-2\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha-2}}}{1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4}\eta^{2}},

confirming (
6.14
).

To verify formula (
6.22
) for
\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}
, start with (
6.8
), that is,

\displaystyle\rho_{\sigma}=\frac{4}{3\kappa(1+\sigma)^{2}}\frac{1}{t^{2}},

to directly obtain

\displaystyle\kappa\rho_{\sigma}\bar{r}^{2}=\kappa\rho\bar{r}^{2}=\frac{4}{3}\alpha^{2}\eta^{2},

confirming (
6.22
).

Finally, to confirm formula (
6.16
) for
v_{\sigma}
, set
k=0
in (
5.38
) to obtain

\displaystyle v_{\sigma}=\dot{R}r=H\bar{r}=\frac{\alpha}{2t}\frac{\bar{r}}{2}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\eta,

which confirms (
6.16
).

It remains to verify expansions (
6.18
)–(
6.23
). Now equations (
6.10
) and (
6.11
) determine
\xi
in terms of
\eta
as

\displaystyle\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}=\frac{\eta}{\mathcal{F}(\eta)}=\eta\bigg(1+\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}\eta^{2}\bigg)^{-\frac{1}{2-\alpha}}.

Using this together with

\displaystyle(1+\gamma\eta^{2})^{\beta}=1+\gamma\beta\eta^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta(\beta-1)\eta^{4}+O(\eta^{6}),

we have

\displaystyle\xi=\eta\bigg(1+\gamma\beta\eta^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{2}\beta(\beta-1)\eta^{4}+O(\eta^{6})\bigg),

with:

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2-\alpha},

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha)}{4}.

Squaring and substituting gives

\displaystyle\xi^{2}=\eta^{2}-\frac{\alpha}{2}\eta^{4}+\frac{1}{16}\alpha^{2}(4-\alpha)\eta^{6}+O(\eta^{8}).

(13.30)

Using the elementary facts:

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=x+ax^{2}+bx^{3}+O(x^{4})

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=y-ay^{2}+(2a^{2}-b)y^{3}+O(y^{4})

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-ax-bx^{2}+O(x^{3})}=1+ax+(a^{2}+b)x^{2}+O(x^{3}),

it is straightforward to invert the series (
13.30
) in a neighborhood of
\xi=\eta=0
to obtain

\displaystyle\eta^{2}=\xi^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\xi^{4}+\frac{\alpha^{3}}{16}\xi^{6}+O(\xi^{8}).

(13.31)

This verifies (
6.18
). Using (
13.31
) in (
6.13
)–(
6.16
) gives (
6.19
)–(
6.22
) and:

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\xi+\frac{\alpha}{4}\xi^{3}+O(\xi^{4}),

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=\eta-\frac{\alpha}{4}\eta^{3}+O(\xi^{4}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\eta)

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha}{4}\eta^{2}+O(\eta^{4}).

Thus we can compute

\displaystyle v_{\sigma}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\eta=\frac{\alpha}{2}\xi\frac{\bar{t}}{t}=\frac{\alpha}{2}\mathcal{F}(\eta)=\frac{\alpha}{2}\xi\Big(1+\frac{\alpha}{4}\xi^{2}+\dots\Big),

as claimed in (
6.23
). This completes the proof of Theorem
30
.∎

13.3
Proof of Theorem
32
: Derivation of the STV-PDE

By
[
12
]
, it suffices to show that (
7.18
)–(
7.21
) are equivalent to (
7.1
), (
7.3
), (
7.4
) and (
7.5
). Neglecting bars, we first convert (
7.1
) and (
7.3
) to a system in
(t,\xi)
to obtain (
7.18
) and (
7.19
) respectively. Since we have:

\displaystyle f_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}f_{\xi},

(13.32)

\displaystyle f_{t}(t,r)

\displaystyle=f_{t}(t,\xi)-\frac{\xi}{t}f_{\xi}(t,\xi),

(13.33)

where
f_{t}(t,\xi)
denotes the partial of
f
with respect to
t
holding
\xi
fixed and
f_{\xi}(t,\xi)
denotes the partial of
f
with respect to
\xi
holding
t
fixed. Making these substitutions into (
7.1
) and (
7.3
) gives the equivalent equations:

\displaystyle\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-z+(1-A),

\displaystyle\xi\frac{B_{\xi}}{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-A}{A}+\frac{1}{A}T^{11}_{M},

respectively. To obtain the implied equation for
D=\sqrt{AB}
, write

\displaystyle 2\xi DD_{\xi}=\xi(AB)_{\xi}=\xi A_{\xi}B+\xi B_{\xi}A,

so

\displaystyle\xi D_{\xi}=\frac{\xi A_{\xi}B+\xi B_{\xi}A}{2D}

and

\displaystyle\xi D_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2D}\bigg((-z+(1-A))B+BA\frac{1-A}{A}+\frac{BA}{A}T^{11}_{M}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{B}{2D}\left(-z+2(1-A)+T^{11}_{M}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{2A}\left(2(1-A)-z+T^{11}_{M}\right).

(13.34)

Putting (
7.9
) into (
13.34
) gives (
7.18
). Reversing these steps verifies the equivalence of (
7.18
) and (
7.19
) with (
7.18
) and (
7.23
) and (
7.1
) and (
7.3
).

It remains now only to prove that (
7.20
) and (
7.21
) are equivalent to (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) respectively, assuming (
7.18
) and (
7.19
). To start, assume
c=1
and multiply equations (
7.4
) and (
7.5
) through by
r^{2}
to get:

\displaystyle\big(T^{00}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}+r^{2}\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{01}_{M}\big)_{r}+2r\sqrt{AB}T^{01}_{M}

\displaystyle=0,

(13.35)

\displaystyle\big(T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}+r^{2}\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{11}_{M}\big)_{r}+2r\sqrt{AB}\big(T^{11}_{M}-T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

(13.36)

with

\displaystyle\{\cdot\}_{*}=\bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{A}-1\bigg)\big(T^{00}_{M}-T^{11}_{M}\big)+\frac{2\kappa r}{A}\big(T^{00}_{M}T^{11}_{M}-(T^{01}_{M})^{2}\big)\bigg\}_{*}.

Combining terms, (
13.35
) becomes

\displaystyle\big(T^{00}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}+\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=0.

(13.37)

To achieve a similar simplification in (
13.36
), add and subtract to get

\displaystyle\big(T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}+r^{2}\Big(\sqrt{AB}\big(T^{11}_{M}-T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)\Big)_{r}+2r\sqrt{AB}\big(T^{11}_{M}-T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)+r^{2}\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

so the second and third terms combine to give

\displaystyle\big(T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}+\Big(\sqrt{AB}\big(T^{11}_{M}r^{2}-T^{22}_{M}r^{4}\big)\Big)_{r}+r^{2}\big(\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

or equivalently

\displaystyle t\big(T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{t}-\xi\big(T^{01}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{\xi}+\Big(D\big(T^{11}_{M}r^{2}-T^{22}_{M}r^{4}\big)\Big)_{\xi}+r^{2}\big(DT^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{\xi}=-\frac{1}{2}tr^{2}D\{\cdot\}_{*}.

(13.38)

Then by (
7.15
) and (
7.16
), together with (
7.7
)–(
7.10
), we have:

\displaystyle\kappa T^{00}_{M}r^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa\rho r^{2}\frac{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}{1-v^{2}}=z,

\displaystyle\kappa T^{01}_{M}r^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}vz=\bar{v}z,

\displaystyle\bar{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\sigma^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}=\xi zw,

\displaystyle\kappa T^{11}_{M}r^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}+v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z,

\displaystyle\kappa T^{22}r^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa\sigma^{2}\rho,

\displaystyle\kappa T^{22}r^{4}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z,

and

\displaystyle\kappa T^{11}_{M}r^{2}-\kappa T^{22}r^{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}+v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z-\frac{\sigma^{2}-\sigma^{2}v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\sigma^{2})v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z=\bar{v}vz

\displaystyle=\bar{v}^{2}z+\bar{v}(v-\bar{v})z

\displaystyle=\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(1-\sigma^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z.

Moreover,

\displaystyle(\bar{v}z)_{t}+\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}z\frac{\kappa T^{11}_{M}r^{2}-\kappa T^{22}r^{4}}{\bar{v}^{2}z}\bigg)_{r}+r^{2}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}\kappa r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*}.

Using these in (
13.37
) and (
13.38
) yields the equivalent system:

\displaystyle z_{t}

\displaystyle+\big(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z\big)_{r}=0,

(13.39)

\displaystyle(\bar{v}z)_{t}

\displaystyle+\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(1-\sigma^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r}+r^{2}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}\kappa r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*}.

(13.40)

We take (
13.39
) as our final form for the
z
equation in terms of
(t,r)
, but for (
13.40
), we use (
13.39
) to write the first term as

\displaystyle(\bar{v}z)_{t}=z\bar{v}_{t}+\bar{v}z_{t}=z\bar{v}_{t}-\bar{v}(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z)_{r}

and the second term in (
13.40
) as

\displaystyle\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(1-\sigma^{2}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r}=\big(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}z\big)_{r}-\sigma^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r},

so that they combine to form

\displaystyle z\bar{v}_{t}+\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z\bar{v}_{r}-\sigma^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1+v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r}.

Substituting this into (
13.40
) yields

\displaystyle z\bar{v}_{t}+\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z\bar{v}_{r}-\sigma^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r}+r^{2}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}\kappa r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*}.

We conclude that in the case (
7.6
), equations (
13.39
) and (
13.40
) are equivalent to:

\displaystyle z_{t}

\displaystyle+\big(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z\big)_{r}=0,

\displaystyle z\bar{v}_{t}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}z\bar{v}_{r}-\sigma^{2}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{r}+r^{2}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{r}=-\frac{1}{2}\kappa r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

and hence equivalent to (
7.4
) and (
7.5
).

We now convert (
13.39
) and (
13.40
) to a system in
(t,\xi)
. Substituting (
13.32
) and (
13.33
) into (
13.39
) directly gives (
7.20
). For (
7.21
), we use (
13.32
) and (
13.33
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t}(\xi w_{\xi}+w),

(13.41)

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi}{t}(tw_{t}-\xi w_{\xi}-w),

(13.42)

for
w(t,\xi)
. Substituting (
13.41
) and (
13.42
) into (
13.40
) gives

\frac{\xi z}{t}\Big(tw_{t}+(-1+\sqrt{AB}w)\xi w_{\xi}-w+\sqrt{AB}w^{2}\Big)\\
-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{t}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{\xi}+\frac{r^{2}}{t}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{\xi}=-\frac{1}{2}\kappa r^{2}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

which yields

tw_{t}+(-1+\sqrt{AB}w)\xi w_{\xi}-w+\sqrt{AB}w^{2}\\
-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\xi z}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{\xi}+\frac{r^{2}}{\xi z}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{\xi}=-\frac{\kappa tr^{2}}{2\xi z}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*},

(13.43)

and where from (
7.5
) we have

\displaystyle\{\cdot\}_{*}=\bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{A}-1\bigg)\big(T^{00}_{M}-T^{11}_{M}\big)+\frac{2\kappa r}{A}\big(T^{00}_{M}T^{11}_{M}-(T^{01}_{M})^{2}\big)\bigg\}_{*}.

Now

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}}{\xi z}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}\big)_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{t^{2}}\frac{r^{2}}{\xi z}\big(\kappa\sqrt{AB}T^{22}_{M}r^{2}t^{2}\big)_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi}{z}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\sigma^{2}\kappa\rho r^{2}\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\bigg)_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\frac{\xi}{z}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\frac{z}{\xi^{2}}\bigg)_{\xi},

so putting this into (
13.43
) gives

tw_{t}+(-1+\sqrt{AB}w)\xi w_{\xi}-w+\sqrt{AB}w^{2}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\xi z}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\bar{v}^{2}\bigg(\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}}\bigg)z\bigg)_{\xi}\\
+\sigma^{2}\frac{\xi}{z}\bigg(\sqrt{AB}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\frac{z}{\xi^{2}}\bigg)_{\xi}=-\frac{\kappa tr^{2}}{2\xi z}\sqrt{AB}\{\cdot\}_{*}.

(13.44)

Using:

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}-T^{11}_{M}

\displaystyle=(1-\sigma^{2})\rho,

\displaystyle T^{00}_{M}T^{11}_{M}-(T^{01}_{M})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}\rho^{2},

we get

\displaystyle\{\cdot\}_{*}=\bigg\{\frac{1}{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{A}-1\bigg)(1-\sigma^{2})\rho+\frac{2\kappa r}{A}\sigma^{2}\rho^{2}\bigg\}_{*},

so

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa tr^{2}}{\xi z}\{\cdot\}_{*}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{A}-1\bigg)(1-\sigma^{2})\frac{\kappa\rho r^{2}}{z}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\xi^{2}A}\frac{(\kappa\rho r^{2})^{2}}{z}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{\kappa\rho r^{2}}{z}\bigg(\frac{1-A}{A}(1-\sigma^{2})+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{A}\kappa\rho r^{2}\bigg).

Since

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa\rho r^{2}}{z}=\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}},

we have

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa tr^{2}}{\xi z}\{\cdot\}_{*}=\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\bigg(\frac{1-A}{A}(1-\sigma^{2})+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{A}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z\bigg).

Thus the right hand side of equation (
13.44
) is

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\xi^{2}}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}\sqrt{AB}\bigg(\frac{1-A}{A}(1-\sigma^{2})+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{A}\frac{1-v^{2}}{1+\sigma^{2}v^{2}}z\bigg),

which yields (
7.21
).∎

13.4
Proof of Theorem
34
: Smoothness at the Center is Preserved Under Evolution

We start with equations:

\displaystyle\xi A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=(1-A)-z,

(13.45)

\displaystyle\xi D_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{2A}\big(2(1-A)-z+\xi^{2}w^{2}z\big).

(13.46)

\displaystyle tz_{t}

\displaystyle=-\xi\big((-1+Dw)z\big)_{\xi}-Dwz,

(13.47)

\displaystyle tw_{t}

\displaystyle=-\xi(-1+Dw)w_{\xi}+w-D\bigg(w^{2}+\frac{1-A}{2\xi^{2}A}(1-\xi^{2}w^{2})\bigg),

(13.48)

First note that products and quotients of smooth functions that satisfy the condition that all odd derivatives vanish at
\xi=0
also have this property. Now for a function
F(t,\xi)
, let
F^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)
denote the
n^{th}
partial derivative of
F
with respect to
\xi
at
\xi=0
. We prove this theorem by induction on
n
. For this, assume
n\geq 1
is odd and make the induction hypothesis that for all odd
k<n
,
F^{(k)}_{\xi}(t)=0
for all
t\geq t_{0}
and all functions
F=z,w,A,D
. We prove that
F^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0
for
t>t_{0}
. For this, we employ the following simple observation: If the
n^{th}
derivative of the product of
m
functions

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\xi^{n}}(F_{1}\dots F_{m})

is expanded into a sum by the product rule, the only terms that will not have a factor containing an odd derivative of order less than
n
are the terms in which all the derivatives fall on the same factor. This follows from the simple fact that if the sum of
k
integers is odd, then one of them must be odd. Taking the
n^{th}
derivative of (
13.47
) and setting
\xi=0
gives the ODE at
\xi=0

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}z^{(n)}_{\xi}=-n\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\xi^{n}}\big((-1+Dw)z\big)-\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\xi^{n}}(DWz).

(13.49)

Since all odd derivatives of order less than
n
are assumed to vanish at
\xi=0
, we can apply the observation and the assumptions (
7.30
), that
D=1
,
w=w_{0}(t)
and
z=0
at
\xi=0
, to see that only the
n^{th}
order derivative
z^{(n)}_{\xi}
survives on the right hand side of (
13.49
). That is, by the induction hypothesis, (
13.49
) reduces to

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}z^{(n)}_{\xi}=\big(n-(n+1)w_{0}(t)\big)z^{(n)}_{\xi}.

(13.50)

Since under the change of variable
t\to\ln(t)
, (
13.50
) is a linear first order homogeneous ODE in
z^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)
with
z^{(n)}_{\xi}(t_{0})=0
, it follows by uniqueness of solutions that
z^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0
for all
t\geq t_{0}
. This proves the theorem for the solution component
z(t,\xi)
.

Consider next equation (
13.45
). Differentiating both sides
n
times with respect to
\xi
and setting
\xi=0
gives

\displaystyle(n+1)A^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=-z^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0

for
t\geq t_{0}
, thus verifying

\displaystyle A^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0

for
t\geq t_{0}
, which verifies the theorem for component
A
.

Consider now equation (
13.46
). Differentiating both sides
n
times with respect to
\xi
, setting
\xi=0
and applying the observation and the induction hypothesis yields

\displaystyle nD^{(n)}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial\xi^{n}}\bigg(D\frac{1-A}{A}\bigg)

\displaystyle=D^{(n)}_{\xi}\frac{1-A}{A}+D\bigg(\frac{1-A}{A}\bigg)^{(n)}_{\xi}+\sum_{k<n\ \text{odd}}c_{k}D^{(k)}_{\xi}=0

for
t\geq t_{0}
because
A=1
at
\xi=0
, all lower order odd derivatives are assumed to vanish at
\xi=0
and we have already verified the theorem for the component
A
. This proves

\displaystyle D^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0

for
t\geq t_{0}
, verifying the theorem for component
D
.

Consider lastly equation (
13.48
). Differentiating both sides
n
times with respect to
\xi
, setting
\xi=0
and applying our observation gives

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}w^{(n)}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-n(-1+w_{0}(t))w^{(n)}_{\xi}+w^{(n)}_{\xi}-(w^{2})^{(n)}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=-n(-1+w_{0}(t))w^{(n)}_{\xi}+w^{(n)}_{\xi}-2ww^{(n)}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=(-n(-1+w_{0}(t))+1-2w)w^{(n)}_{\xi}

for
t\geq t_{0}
because all lower order odd derivatives are assumed to vanish at
\xi=0
. Thus
w^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)
solves the first order homogeneous ODE

\displaystyle t\frac{d}{dt}w^{(n)}_{\xi}

\displaystyle=(-n(-1+w_{0}(t))+1-2w)w^{(n)}_{\xi}

starting from zero initial data at
t=t_{0}
, so again we conclude

\displaystyle w^{(n)}_{\xi}(t)=0

for
t\geq t_{0}
. This verifies the theorem for the final component
w
, thereby completing the proof of Theorem
34
.∎

13.5
Proof of Theorem
35
: Derivation of the STV-ODE of Order 2

We start with equations (
13.47
) and (
13.48
) in the form:

\displaystyle tz_{t}

\displaystyle=\xi z_{\xi}-\xi(Dwz)_{\xi}-Dwz

(13.51)

\displaystyle tw_{t}

\displaystyle=(1-Dw)(\xi w_{\xi}+w)-D\frac{1-A}{2\xi^{2}A}(1-\xi^{2}w^{2}).

(13.52)

Applying (
8.3
) to terms in (
13.51
) gives:

\displaystyle tz_{t}

\displaystyle=t\dot{z}_{2}\xi^{2}+t\dot{z}_{4}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(13.53)

\displaystyle\xi z_{\xi}

\displaystyle=2z_{2}\xi^{2}+4z_{4}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

(13.54)

and

\displaystyle Dwz=w_{0}z_{2}\xi^{2}+(z_{2}w_{2}+w_{0}z_{4}+D_{2}z_{2}w_{0})\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}).

(13.55)

Thus putting ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) into (
13.51
), using (
13.53
)–(
13.55
) and collecting like powers of
\xi
shows that the equations for the corrections close at every even power of
\xi
and yield the following equations for the corrections at orders
\xi^{2}
and
\xi^{4}
:

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=2z_{2}-3z_{2}w_{0},

(13.56)

\displaystyle t\dot{z}_{4}

\displaystyle=-5D_{2}w_{0}z_{2}+4z_{4}-5w_{0}z_{4}-5w_{2}z_{2}.

(13.57)

Applying the ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) to terms in equation (
13.52
) gives:

\displaystyle tw_{t}

\displaystyle=t\dot{w}_{0}+t\dot{w}_{2}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(13.58)

\displaystyle 1-Dw

\displaystyle=(1-w_{0})-(w_{2}+D_{2}w_{0})\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(13.59)

\displaystyle\xi w_{\xi}+w

\displaystyle=w_{0}+3w_{2}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(13.60)

\displaystyle\frac{1-A}{2\xi^{2}A}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}A_{2}+\frac{1}{2}(A_{2}^{2}-A_{4}^{2})\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

(13.61)

\displaystyle D\frac{1-A}{2\xi^{2}A}(1-\xi^{2}w^{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}A_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\big(A_{2}w_{0}^{2}+A_{2}^{2}-A_{4}-A_{2}D_{2}\big)\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}).

(13.62)

Thus putting ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) into (
13.52
), using (
13.58
)–(
13.62
) and collecting like powers of
\xi
shows that the equations for the corrections close at every even power of
\xi
, and this yields the following equations for the corrections at orders zero and
\xi^{2}
:

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{0}

\displaystyle=w_{0}-w_{0}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A_{2},

(13.63)

\displaystyle t\dot{w}_{2}

\displaystyle=3w_{2}-4w_{0}w_{2}-D_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}A_{2}w_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}A_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}A_{4}+\frac{1}{2}A_{2}D_{2}.

(13.64)

Substituting now the ansatz (
8.1
) and (
8.3
) into (
7.24
) in the form

\displaystyle z=-\big(\xi A_{\xi}+(A-1)\big)

yields

\displaystyle z_{2}\xi^{2}+z_{4}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6})=-3A_{2}\xi^{2}-5A_{4}\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

so equating orders gives

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2},

\displaystyle A_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4}.

(13.65)

Similarly, putting ansatz (
8.1
)–(
8.4
) into (
7.25
) and keeping only
O(\xi^{2})
terms yields

\displaystyle 2D_{2}\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4})=-\frac{1}{2}(2A_{2}+z_{2})\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

so using (
13.65
) and equating orders gives

\displaystyle D_{2}=-\frac{1}{12}z_{2}.

(13.66)

Finally, putting (
13.65
) and (
13.66
) into (
13.56
) and (
13.57
) and (
13.63
) and (
13.64
) yields (
8.13
)–(
8.16
). This completes the proof of Theorem
35
.∎

13.6
Proof of Theorem
42
: The Expansion of Friedmann in SSCNG Case
k=-1

Since the derivation of (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) and (
10.27
)–(
10.30
) is based on known formulas for the
k=\pm 1
Friedmann metric, for the proofs contained in this subsection and the next, we return to our earlier notation of letting unbarred coordinates
(t,r)
denote Friedmann comoving coordinates, and barred coordinates
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
denote SSC.

The solution of the
p=0
,
k=-1
Friedmann equations (
5.4
)–(
5.5
) is given by (
5.23
)–(
5.24
) in Friedmann coordinates
(t,r)
. We write these formulas in the form:

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta),

(13.67)

\displaystyle\frac{R}{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\cosh 2\theta-1),

(13.68)

where by (
5.17
) the Friedmann free parameter
\Delta_{0}
is taken to be

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa}{3}\rho R^{3}.

To transform these coordinates over to SSCNG, we begin by finding a simple expression for
\bar{t}=h^{-1}(h(t)g(r))
in (
5.31
), where
h
and
g
are given by (
5.33
) to be:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d\tau}{\dot{R}(\tau)R(\tau)}},

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=(1+r^{2})^{\frac{\lambda}{2}},

where
R(t)
is defined implicitly by (
5.23
) and (
5.24
). To obtain expressions in terms of
\theta
, we calculate the following:

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{d\theta}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}(\cosh 2\theta-1),

(13.69)

\displaystyle\dot{R}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}\frac{d\theta}{dt}\sinh 2\theta=\coth\theta,

(13.70)

\displaystyle\frac{d\dot{R}}{d\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{\cosh 2\theta-1}=-\csch^{2}\theta,

(13.71)

\displaystyle\ddot{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{d\dot{R}}{d\theta}\frac{d\theta}{dt}=-\frac{\csch^{2}2\theta}{\Delta_{0}(\cosh 2\theta-1)},

(13.72)

\displaystyle\dot{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ddot{R}R-\dot{R}^{2}}{R^{2}}=-\frac{4(\sinh^{2}2\theta+\cosh 2\theta-1)}{\Delta_{0}^{2}(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{4}}.

(13.73)

Note first that by (
13.70
), (
13.67
) and (
13.68
), we have

\displaystyle\dot{R}R=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}\sinh 2\theta,

and using this in (
13.70
) gives a formula for
\theta
in terms of
t
, namely

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{1}{\Delta_{0}}(R\dot{R}-t).

Using this in (
5.40
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\dot{R}R}=2\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\Delta_{0}\sinh 2\theta(s)}.

Now let

\displaystyle s=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta),

so

\displaystyle ds=\Delta_{0}(\cosh 2\theta-1)d\theta,

and substitution yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\dot{R}(s)R(s)}

\displaystyle=2\int_{t=0}^{t}\frac{\cosh 2\theta-1}{\sinh 2\theta}d\theta=2\int_{t=0}^{t}\frac{2\sinh^{2}\theta}{2\sinh\theta\cosh\theta}d\theta

\displaystyle=2\int_{t=0}^{t}\frac{\sinh\theta}{\cosh\theta}d\theta=\Big[2\ln|\cosh\theta|\Big]_{t=0}^{t}=\cosh^{2}\theta.

Using this in (
5.40
) gives

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=\Big[e^{\lambda\ln|\cosh^{2}\theta|}\Big]_{t=0}^{t}=\cosh^{2\lambda}\theta.

Taking
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
now gives:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=\cosh\theta(t),

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}},

\displaystyle h^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\circ\cosh^{-1}(y).

So since we started with (
5.23
) and
\Phi(t,r)=h(t)g(r)
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{t}=h^{-1}(\Phi)=\theta^{-1}\Big(\cosh^{-1}\big(\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\cosh\theta(t)\big)\Big),

or equivalently

\displaystyle\cosh\theta(\bar{t})=\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\cosh\theta(t).

Thus in summary,
\bar{t}=F(h(t)g(r))
with
F(y)=h^{-1}(y)
, so the coordinate transformation from
(t,r)\to(\bar{t},\bar{r})
is:

\displaystyle\bar{t}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\circ\cosh^{-1}\big(\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\cosh\theta(t)\big),

(13.74)

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=R(t)r.

(13.75)

Our goal is to derive (
10.19
)–(
10.22
) by writing
A
,
z
and
w
as functions of
(\bar{t},\xi)
for
0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
using the
k=-1
Friedmann formulas and then determine the Taylor coefficients of the expansion in
\xi
.

We begin by deriving formulas for
A_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}z_{2}
and
A_{4}=-\frac{1}{5}z_{4}
. Start with equation (
5.59
). We find
A_{2}(\bar{t})
and
A_{4}(\bar{t})
such that

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=A_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+A_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}).

Note that from (
5.59
) we have

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=1-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R(t)^{3}}\xi^{2},

(13.76)

where
t=t(\bar{t},\bar{r})
needs to be expressed as a function of
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
. Since
t=\bar{t}+O(\xi^{2})
, it follows immediately from (
13.76
) that

\displaystyle A_{2}(\bar{t})=-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{3}(\bar{t})}.

(13.77)

Now since
A_{2}
is a function of
\bar{t}
and
\bar{t}=t+O(\xi^{2})
to within the order we seek, we can write
A_{2}(\bar{t})
as a function of
\theta
at
\xi=0
by identifying

\displaystyle\bar{t}=t=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)

from (
13.67
). The result is

\displaystyle A_{2}(\bar{t})=-\frac{2(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{2}}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{3}},

(13.78)

which follows directly from (
13.67
) and (
13.68
). This establishes (
10.19
).

We now find
A_{4}(\bar{t})
in the case
k=-1
. Writing

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=1-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{3}(t(\bar{t},\xi))}\xi^{2}=:1+a(\bar{t},t(\bar{t},\xi))\xi^{2},

(13.79)

together with

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=1+A_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+A_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

we derive
A_{4}(\bar{t})
by Taylor expanding
a(\bar{t},t(\bar{t},\xi))
in
\xi
, using a formula for

\displaystyle t_{\xi\xi}:=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\xi^{2}}t(\bar{t},\xi),

obtained implicitly from the transformation law (
13.74
)–(
13.75
) by writing

\displaystyle\cosh\theta(\bar{t})=\sqrt[4]{1+\frac{\bar{t}^{2}\xi^{2}}{R^{2}(t)}}\cosh\theta(t).

(13.80)

That is, we take (
13.80
) as the implicit definition for
t=t(\bar{t},\xi)
. Differentiating (
13.79
) with respect to
\xi
holding
\bar{t}
fixed gives:

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=1+a\xi^{2},

\displaystyle A_{\xi}

\displaystyle=a_{\xi}\xi^{2}+2a\xi,

\displaystyle A_{\xi\xi}

\displaystyle=a_{\xi\xi}\xi^{2}+4a_{\xi}\xi+2a,

\displaystyle A_{\xi\xi\xi}

\displaystyle=a_{\xi\xi\xi}\xi^{2}+6a_{\xi\xi}\xi+6a_{\xi},

\displaystyle A_{\xi\xi\xi\xi}

\displaystyle=a_{\xi\xi\xi\xi}\xi^{2}+8a_{\xi\xi\xi}\xi+12a_{\xi\xi}.

Thus

\displaystyle A_{4}=\frac{1}{4!}A_{\xi\xi\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=\frac{1}{2}a_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0).

Differentiating

\displaystyle a(\bar{t},\xi)=-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{3}(t)}

with respect to
\xi
holding
\bar{t}
fixed yields:

\displaystyle a_{\xi}

\displaystyle=3\Delta_{0}\frac{\dot{R}(t)}{R^{4}(t)}t_{\xi}\bar{t}^{2},

\displaystyle a_{\xi\xi}

\displaystyle=3\Delta_{0}\frac{\dot{R}(t)}{R^{4}(t)}t_{\xi\xi}\bar{t}^{2}-12\Delta_{0}\frac{\dot{R}(t)}{R^{5}(t)}t_{\xi}^{2}\bar{t}^{2}+3\Delta_{0}\frac{\dot{R}(t)}{R^{4}(t)}t_{\xi}^{2}\bar{t}^{2}.

Setting
\xi=0
and using
t_{\xi}(\bar{t},0)=0
and
\bar{t}=t
at
r=\xi=0
gives

\displaystyle a_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=3\Delta_{0}\frac{\dot{R}(\bar{t})}{R^{4}(\bar{t})}t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\bar{t}^{2}.

It remains to find
t_{\xi\xi}
and
\dot{R}(\bar{t})
as functions of
(\bar{t},\xi)
at
\xi=0
. First write (
13.80
) as

\displaystyle\cosh\theta(\bar{t})=\sqrt[4]{b(\bar{t},\xi)}\cosh\theta(t)

(13.81)

with

\displaystyle b(\bar{t},\xi)=1+r^{2}=1+\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}(t)}\xi^{2}.

(13.82)

Differentiating (
13.81
) with respect to
\xi
with
\bar{t}
fixed gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}b^{-\frac{3}{4}}b_{\xi}\cosh\theta(t)+b^{\frac{1}{4}}t_{\xi}\theta^{\prime}(t)\sinh\theta(t)=0.

Differentiating again, setting
\xi=0
and using:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\bar{t}+O(\xi^{2}),

\displaystyle\dot{R}(t)

\displaystyle=\dot{R}(\bar{t})+O(\xi),

together with:

\displaystyle b(\bar{t},0)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle b_{\xi}

\displaystyle:=b_{\xi}(\bar{t},0)=0,

\displaystyle t_{\xi}:=t_{\xi}(\bar{t},0)

\displaystyle=0,

(noting that we are changing definitions) gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}b_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\cosh\theta(\bar{t})+t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\theta^{\prime}(\bar{t})\sinh\theta(\bar{t})=0.

So from the definition of
b
in (
13.82
),

\displaystyle b_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=\frac{2\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}(\bar{t})}.

From this we conclude

\displaystyle t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=-\frac{\cosh\theta(\bar{t})}{2\theta^{\prime}(\bar{t})\sinh\theta(\bar{t})}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}(\bar{t})}.

(13.83)

By (
13.67
), since
\xi=0
, we have

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}(\bar{t})=\frac{1}{\Delta_{0}(\cosh 2\theta(\bar{t})-1)},

so putting this into (
13.83
), and using
\theta=\theta(\bar{t})
, gives

\displaystyle t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{0}(\cosh 2\theta-1)\cosh\theta}{2\sinh\theta}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}(\bar{t})}

(13.84)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{0}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{2}\cosh\theta}{2(\cosh 2\theta-1)\sinh\theta}.

(13.85)

Thus we have

\displaystyle A_{4}(\bar{t},0)=\frac{1}{2}a_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=\frac{3\Delta_{0}}{2}\frac{\dot{R}(\bar{t})}{R^{4}(\bar{t})}t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\bar{t}^{2}.

(13.86)

Note that (
13.86
) holds for any
k=\pm 1
and will be used for the case
k=+1
below as well. Using (
13.77
) and (
13.84
) in (
13.86
) gives

\displaystyle A_{4}(\bar{t},0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\Delta_{0}^{2}}{4}\bigg(\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{3}(\bar{t})}\bigg)^{2}\dot{R}(\bar{t})\frac{(\cosh 2\theta-1)\cosh\theta}{\sinh\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4}A_{2}(\bar{t})^{2}\dot{R}(\bar{t})\frac{(\cosh 2\theta-1)\cosh\theta}{\sinh\theta}.

We summarize this as

\displaystyle A_{4}(\bar{t},0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{6(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{4}\cosh^{2}\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{6}}.

(13.87)

Equation (
13.87
) follows from (
13.77
) and (
13.70
) because
A_{4}(\bar{t})
is computed at
\xi=0
where
\bar{t}=t
. This establishes (
10.21
).

We next obtain formulas for
w_{0}
and
w_{2}
by expanding
w=\frac{v}{\xi}
in even powers of
\xi
. We begin with the formula (
5.38
) for
v
,

\displaystyle w=\frac{v}{\xi}=\frac{1}{\xi}\frac{\dot{R}(t)r}{\sqrt{1-kr^{2}}}.

(13.88)

Substituting
k=-1
,
\bar{r}=R(t)r
and
\xi=\frac{\bar{r}}{\bar{t}}
into (
13.88
) gives the exact formula

\displaystyle w=\frac{\dot{R}(t)\bar{t}}{R(t)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}(t)}\xi^{2}}}.

(13.89)

Now we know
t=t(\bar{t},\xi)
and
t
agrees with
\bar{t}
at
\xi=0
, so
t=t(\bar{t},0)
with error order
O(\xi^{2})
, so we again write

\displaystyle t=\bar{t}+f(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}),

where
2f(\bar{t})=t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)
and, according to (
13.85
),

\displaystyle t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=-\frac{\Delta_{0}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{2}\cosh\theta}{2(\cosh 2\theta-1)\sinh\theta}.

Thus

\displaystyle w(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=H(t)\bar{t}\bigg(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R(t)^{2}}\xi^{2}\bigg)+O(\xi^{4})

\displaystyle=H\big(\bar{t}+f(\bar{t})\xi^{2}\big)\bar{t}\bigg(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R(t)^{2}}\xi^{2}\bigg)+O(\xi^{4})

\displaystyle=\bigg(H(\bar{t})+\frac{1}{2}H_{\xi\xi}\xi^{2}\bigg)\bar{t}\bigg(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R(t)^{2}}\xi^{2}\bigg)+O(\xi^{4}),

where we have written
H=H(t(\bar{t},\xi))
, so

\displaystyle H_{\xi}=\dot{H}t_{\xi},

and since
t_{\xi}=O(\xi)
we have to leading even orders

\displaystyle H_{\xi\xi}(t(\bar{t},0))=H_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t})=2\dot{H}f(\bar{t})=\dot{H}(\bar{t})t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0).

Continuing, we obtain

\displaystyle w(\bar{t},\xi)

\displaystyle=\Big(H(\bar{t})+\dot{H}(\bar{t})t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\xi^{2}\Big)\bar{t}\bigg(1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R(\bar{t})^{2}}\xi^{2}\bigg)+O(\xi^{4})

\displaystyle=H(\bar{t})\bar{t}+\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\dot{H}(\bar{t})t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\bar{t}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{H(\bar{t})\bar{t}^{3}}{R(\bar{t})^{2}}\bigg)\xi^{2}+O(\xi^{4}).

From this we obtain the following exact formulas valid at
\xi=0
for
k=-1
and
k=+1
:

\displaystyle w_{0}

\displaystyle=H(\bar{t})\bar{t}

(13.90)

\displaystyle w_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\dot{H}(\bar{t})t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)\bar{t}-\frac{H(\bar{t})\bar{t}^{3}}{2R(\bar{t})^{2}}.

(13.91)

Using formulas (
13.67
)–(
13.73
) together with
\bar{t}=t(\bar{t},0)
we immediately obtain

\displaystyle w_{0}=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)\sinh 2\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{2}}.

This establishes (
10.20
).

To express
w_{2}
as a function of
\theta
, write (
13.91
) as

\displaystyle w_{2}=w_{2}^{B}-w_{2}^{A},

(13.92)

where

\displaystyle w_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{H\bar{t}^{3}}{2R^{2}},

(13.93)

\displaystyle w_{2}^{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\dot{H}t_{\xi\xi}\bar{t},

(13.94)

and where the functions
R
,
H
,
\dot{H}
and
t_{\xi\xi}
each take
\bar{t}
as their only argument, since we evaluate at
\xi=0
. Substituting (
13.67
), (
13.68
) and (
13.73
) into (
13.93
) and simplifying gives

\displaystyle w_{2}^{A}=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{3}\cosh\theta}{(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{4}}\sinh\theta,

(13.95)

which uses the expression

\displaystyle\frac{\bar{t}^{3}}{R^{2}(\bar{t})}=\frac{\Delta_{0}(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{3}}{2(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{4}}.

To write
w_{2}^{B}
as a function of
\theta
, substitute (
13.67
), (
13.68
), (
13.73
) and (
13.85
) into (
13.94
) and simplify to obtain

\displaystyle w_{2}^{B}=\frac{(\sinh 2\theta-2\theta)^{3}\cosh\theta}{2(\cosh 2\theta-1)^{5}\sinh\theta}(\sinh^{2}2\theta+\cosh 2\theta-1).

(13.96)

Putting formulas (
13.95
) and (
13.96
) for
w_{2}^{A}
and
w_{2}^{B}
into (
13.92
) establishes (
10.22
). This competes the proof of Theorem
42
in the case
k=-1
.∎

13.7
Proof of Theorem
42
: The Expansion of Friedmann in SSCNG Case
k=+1

We continue in this subsection with our original notation that unbarred coordinates
(t,r)
denote Friedmann comoving coordinates and barred coordinates
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
denote SSC. To establish (
10.27
)–(
10.30
), we begin with formulas (
5.27
)–(
5.28
) for the solutions of the Friedmann equations (
5.4
)–(
5.5
), given in Friedmann coordinates
(t,r)
, for
p=0
,
k=+1
, written in the form:

\displaystyle\frac{t}{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(2\theta-\sin 2\theta),

(13.97)

\displaystyle\frac{R}{\Delta_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1-\cos 2\theta),

(13.98)

where again
\Delta_{0}
is the Friedmann free parameter given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{0}=\frac{\kappa}{3}\rho R^{3}.

As in the case
k=-1
, to transform these coordinates over to SSCNG, we begin by finding a simple expression for
\bar{t}=h^{-1}(h(t)g(r))
, where
f
and
g
are given in (
5.33
) for case
k=+1
by:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda\int_{0}^{t}\frac{d\tau}{\dot{R}(\tau)R(\tau)}},

(13.99)

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=(1-r^{2})^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}.

(13.100)

To obtain expressions in terms of
\theta
, we calculate the following:

\displaystyle\frac{dt}{d\theta}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}(1-\cos 2\theta),

(13.101)

\displaystyle\dot{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}R(\theta(t))=\frac{dR}{d\theta}\frac{d\theta}{dt}=\frac{\sin 2\theta}{1-\cos 2\theta}=2\cot\theta,

(13.102)

\displaystyle\frac{d\dot{R}}{d\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\sin^{2}\theta}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{2}}=-\csc^{2}\theta,

(13.103)

\displaystyle\ddot{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{d\dot{R}}{d\theta}\frac{d\theta}{dt}=-\frac{2}{\Delta_{0}(1-\cos 2\theta)^{2}}=-\frac{1}{\Delta_{0}}\csc^{4}\theta,

(13.104)

and

\displaystyle\dot{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ddot{R}R-\dot{R}^{2}}{R^{2}}=-\frac{4(1-\cos 2\theta+\sin^{2}2\theta)}{\Delta_{0}(1-\cos 2\theta)^{4}}.

(13.105)

Note first that by (
13.98
) and (
13.97
) we have

\displaystyle\dot{R}R=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}\sin 2\theta,

and using this in (
13.97
) gives a formula for
\theta
in terms of
t
, namely

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{1}{\Delta_{0}}(R\dot{R}+t).

Using this in (
13.99
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\dot{R}(s)R(s)}=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{2ds}{\Delta_{0}\sin 2\theta(s)}.

Making the substitution:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}(2\theta-\sin 2\theta),

\displaystyle ds

\displaystyle=\Delta_{0}(1-\cos 2\theta)d\theta,

gives, noting (
13.101
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{\dot{R}(s)R(s)}=2\int_{t=0}^{t}\frac{2(1-\cos 2\theta)}{\sin 2\theta}d\theta=2\int_{t=0}^{t}\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}d\theta=-2\ln|\cos\theta(t)|.

Using this in (
13.99
) then gives

\displaystyle h(t)=e^{-2\lambda\ln|\cos 2\theta(t)|}=\sec^{2\lambda}\theta(t).

Now taking
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
we obtain:

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle=\sec\theta(t),

(13.106)

\displaystyle g(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}},

(13.107)

\displaystyle h^{-1}(y)

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\circ\sec^{-1}(y).

(13.108)

Thus by (
13.106
)–(
13.108
) and
\Phi(t,r)=h(t)g(r)
, we have

\displaystyle\bar{t}=h^{-1}(\Phi)=\theta^{-1}\Big(\sec^{-1}\big(\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\sec\theta(t)\big)\Big)

or

\displaystyle\sec\theta(\bar{t})=\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\sec\theta(t).

(13.109)

Thus in summary,
\bar{t}=F(h(t)g(r))
with
F(y)=h^{-1}(y)
, so the coordinate transformation from
(t,r)\to(\bar{t},\bar{r})
with normalized gauge is:

\displaystyle\bar{t}

\displaystyle=\theta^{-1}\circ\sec^{-1}\big(\sqrt[4]{1+r^{2}}\sec\theta(t)\big),

(13.110)

\displaystyle\bar{r}

\displaystyle=R(t)r.

(13.111)

Note that the coordinate transformation (
13.110
) becomes singular at
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
, the point where the
k=+1
Friedmann spacetime expands to its maximum. Thus for
0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
, the transformation (
13.110
)–(
13.111
) describes the
k=+1
Friedmann spacetime in SSC from the Big Bang
\theta=t=0
, out to the maximum
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
,
\bar{t}=\Delta_{0}(\frac{\pi}{2}-1)
. Our goal is to write
A
,
z
and
w
as functions of
(\bar{t},\xi)
for
0\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
for the
k=+1
Friedmann solution and confirm that the Taylor coefficients of the expansion in
\xi
are given by (
10.27
)–(
10.30
).

To derive
A_{2}(\bar{t})
and
A_{4}(\bar{t})
such that

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=A_{2}(\bar{t})\xi^{2}+A_{4}(\bar{t})\xi^{4}+O(\xi^{6}),

we start with equation (
5.59
). Note that from (
5.59
) we have

\displaystyle A(\bar{t},\xi)=1-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R(t)^{3}}\xi^{2},

(13.112)

where again,
t=t(\bar{t},\bar{r})
needs to be expressed as a function of
(\bar{t},\bar{r})
. Since
t=\bar{t}+O(\xi^{2})
, it follows immediately from (
13.112
) that

\displaystyle A_{2}(\bar{t})=-\frac{\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{3}(\bar{t})}.

Now since
A_{2}
is a function of
\bar{t}
and
\bar{t}=t+O(\xi^{2})
to within the order we seek, we can write
A_{2}(\bar{t})
as a function of
\theta
at
\xi=0
by identifying

\displaystyle\bar{t}=t=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{2}(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)

from (
13.97
). The result is

\displaystyle A_{2}(\bar{t})=-\frac{2(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{2}}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{3}},

(13.113)

which follows directly from (
13.97
) and (
13.98
). From (
13.113
) we obtain the limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}A_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{9},

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}A_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{4},

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}A_{2}

\displaystyle=\infty.

Equations (
10.27
) and the second limit in (
10.35
) follow from the identity
z_{2}=-3A_{2}
, see (
13.65
). We next find
w_{0}
. For this, we start with (
13.90
), given by

\displaystyle w_{0}=H(\bar{t})\bar{t}=\frac{\dot{R}(\bar{t})}{R(\bar{t})}\bar{t}.

Substituting the
k=+1
formulas (
13.97
), (
13.98
) and (
13.102
) into the right hand side gives

\displaystyle w_{0}=\frac{(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)\sin 2\theta}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{2}}.

(13.114)

From (
13.114
) we obtain the limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}w_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}w_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}w_{0}

\displaystyle=-\infty.

This establishes the second limits in (
10.33
) and (
10.35
). To express
z_{4}
and
w_{2}
as functions of
\theta
for the case
k=+1
, we need a formula for
t_{\xi\xi}:=t_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)
. For this, we start with (
13.110
) in the form

\displaystyle\sqrt[4]{b(\bar{t},\xi)}\sec\theta(\bar{t})=\sec\theta(t),

with

\displaystyle b(\bar{t},\xi)=1-\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}}\xi^{2}.

(13.115)

By (
13.115
),

\displaystyle b_{\xi\xi}:=b_{\xi\xi}(\bar{t},0)=-\frac{2\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}}.

Differentiating (
13.109
) twice with respect to
\xi
, setting
\xi=0
and using
b_{\xi}=t_{\xi}=0
at
\xi=0
gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}b_{\xi\xi}=t_{\xi\xi}\dot{\theta}\tan\theta,

so by (
13.97
), (
13.98
), (
13.115
) and (
13.101
) we have

\displaystyle t_{\xi\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}b_{\xi\xi}\frac{dt}{d\theta}\cot\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{0}}{4}(1-\cos 2\theta)\left(-2\frac{\bar{t}^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\frac{\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta_{0}(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{2}\cos\theta}{2(1-\cos 2\theta)\sin\theta}.

(13.116)

Consider now
A_{4}
in the case
k=+1
. We start with (
13.86
). Using (
13.97
), (
13.98
), (
13.115
) and (
13.102
) in (
13.86
) gives

\displaystyle A_{4}=\frac{3\Delta_{0}\bar{t}^{2}\dot{R}(\bar{t})t_{\xi\xi}}{2R^{4}(\bar{t})}=-\frac{6(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{4}\cos^{2}\theta}{(1-\cos 2\theta)^{6}}.

(13.117)

From (
13.117
) we obtain the limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}A_{4}

\displaystyle=-\frac{8}{27},

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}A_{4}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}A_{4}

\displaystyle=-\infty.

Since
z_{4}=-5A_{4}
, this establishes the third limits in (
10.33
) and (
10.35
).

Finally, consider
w_{2}
in the case
k=+1
. We start with (
13.86
). Using (
13.97
), (
13.98
), (
13.115
) and (
13.102
) in (
13.86
) gives

\displaystyle w_{2}=w_{2}^{B}-w_{2}^{A},

(13.118)

where:

\displaystyle w_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{H\bar{t}^{3}}{2R^{2}},

(13.119)

\displaystyle w_{2}^{B}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\dot{H}t_{\xi\xi}\bar{t}.

(13.120)

Substituting (
13.97
), (
13.98
) and (
13.102
) into (
13.119
) gives

\displaystyle w_{2}^{A}=\frac{\dot{R}\bar{t}^{3}}{2R^{3}}=\frac{(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{3}\sin 2\theta}{2(1-\cos 2\theta)^{4}}

(13.121)

and substituting (
13.97
), (
13.105
) and (
13.116
) into (
13.120
) gives

\displaystyle w_{2}^{B}=\frac{\cos\theta(1-\cos 2\theta+\sin^{2}2\theta)(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{3}}{2(1-\cos 2\theta)^{5}\sin\theta}.

(13.122)

From (
13.121
) we obtain the limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}w_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}w_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}w_{2}^{A}

\displaystyle=-\infty,

and from (
13.117
) we obtain the limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}w_{2}^{B}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}w_{2}^{B}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}w_{2}^{B}

\displaystyle=-\infty.

Finally, putting (
13.121
) and (
13.122
) into (
13.118
) gives the formula

\displaystyle w_{2}=\frac{(2\theta-\sin 2\theta)^{3}\cos\theta}{2(1-\cos 2\theta)^{5}\sin\theta}\big(\sin^{2}2\theta+(1-2\sin^{2}\theta)(1-\cos 2\theta)\big).

(13.123)

From (
13.123
) we obtain the limits

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to 0}w_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{9},

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\frac{\pi}{2}}w_{2}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\theta\to\pi^{-}}w_{2}

\displaystyle=-\infty.

This establishes (
10.30
), as well as (
10.33
) and (
10.35
), and thus completes the proof of Theorem
42
and Corollary
43
in the case
k>0
, thereby completing the proof of Theorem
41
.∎

13.8
Proof of Theorem
44
: Derivation of the STV-ODE of Order
n

To show (
11.5
), we start with equation
(
7.26
) in the form

\displaystyle tz_{t}=\xi z_{\xi}-\xi(zwD)_{\xi}-zwD

and expand the terms separately. We first note that

\displaystyle tz_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\dot{z}_{2n}\xi^{2n},

\displaystyle\xi z_{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2nz_{2n}\xi^{2n},

and

\displaystyle zwD=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(\sum_{i+j+k=n}z_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k}\Bigg)\xi^{2n}.

Thus

\displaystyle\xi(zwD)_{\xi}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}2n\Bigg(\sum_{i+j+k=n}z_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k}\Bigg)\xi^{2n}

and

\displaystyle\xi z_{\xi}-\xi(zwD)_{\xi}-zwD=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(2nz_{2n}-(2n+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=n\\
i\neq n,\,j\neq n-1\end{subarray}}z_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k}\Bigg)\xi^{2n}.

(13.124)

Using this in (
7.26
) gives (
11.5
). Extracting the leading order term first in equation (
13.124
) we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}t\dot{z}_{2n}\xi^{2n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Big(\big((2n+1)(1-w_{0})-1\big)z_{2n}-(2n+1)z_{2}w_{2n-2}\Big)\xi^{2n}

\displaystyle-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Bigg((2n+1)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j+k=n\\
i\neq n,\,j\neq n-1\end{subarray}}z_{2i}w_{2j}D_{2k}\Bigg)\xi^{2n},

(13.125)

and so this confirms the first row of the matrix (
44
).

To show (
11.6
), we write equation (
7.27
) in the form

\displaystyle tw_{t}=\frac{1}{2}(1-w^{2}\xi^{2})\frac{A-1}{\xi^{2}A}D+(\xi w_{\xi}+w)(1-wD)

(13.126)

and again expand the terms separately. To expand the first term on the right hand side of (
13.126
), we write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1-w^{2}\xi^{2})\frac{A-1}{\xi^{2}A}D=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\hat{w}_{2i}a_{2j}D_{2k}\bigg)\xi^{2n},
